WOMAN'S WORLD.

• . 'CHAT and GOSSIP.

r

p

of cholera, and the diseases that follow localised distress, are of such frequent occurrence that the women are constantly employed in relief work of one kind or another. Flower Day is a Russian idea, the sale of marigolds assists con- sumptives, lilies of the valley children's creches and homes, wheatears famine funds; and a Flag Day recently realised a very large sum for the many war funds.

The Vogue for Black Velvet. Black velvet reigns supreme in the domain of dressmakers and milliners, for it strikes a rich and dignified note, in keeping with this winter's demands. Strangely enough, it never becomes monotonous, either in gowns or in hats, although six out of every seven of the latter are made of it. Many new makes of velvet have appeared quite lately; all are re- markable for their extreme lightness and soft- ness, and these qualities permit of the material being accordion pleated with good effect, and the greatest facility. Velvet in different shades of gold is used for afternoon and evening toilettes intended for ceremonious occasions. The Princess dress has returned with the vogue for velvet and no style is better suited to this fabric. Many women will welcome its return, for it is particularly beciming to full figures; at the same time it suits the slender, elegant women equally well. These Princess dresses are relieved with touches of ermine, or lace, and are altogether desirable. Genet and Black Fox.

Natural genet is an old-fashioned fur that goes remarkably well with black velvet, and this fact doubtless accounts for its return to favour. Stoles this winter have usuqlly round ends, and these are repeated along The bottom edge of the muffs. Tails are not as much worn , as of late, but they accompany all sets made 1, of black fox. The latter fur is never out of fashion, but just at present, when so much black is seen, it is found to add to the sombreness of the toilette, and is in consequence relegated to a secondary position in the world of paltry. But it is too becoming to remain long in even semi- obscurity, and as a trimming for the white cloth tailormades, that have been so popular during the mild autumn weather, it proved alto- gether fascinating. White fox suits blonde and brunette, and for those who can afford the

cost it is an ideal fur. A new muff has a broad belt centre, which gives it a contrast in colour; for instance, a tailless ermine has a band of civet cat; the effect is novel rather than good. There is very little change in the shape of muffs, and last year's designs are still shown.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
By TAARJORIE.

Royal Mourning. Several soldiers of Royal birth have already fallen in the war, and the death of Prince Maurice of Battenberg has not only placed the Court in mourning, but has cast a gloom over the Army, where he was liked by all who knew him. He was instinctively a soldier, an excel- lent marksman, and was one of the Wellington Shooting Eight that competed for the Ashburn- -ham Shield. As a schoolboy he was noted for his pluck and love of fun, which developed into a soldier's disdain of danger. Four years younger than Her Majesty Queen Victoria Eugenie, he was her companion in a greater degree than his elder brothers, and the sym- pathy of every woman will go out to his mother, Princess Henry, and to his sister, the Queen of Spain, in their bereavement.

Fewer Operations. It is curious that a slump in ordinary surgi- cal operations should have followed the out- break of hostilities; but such is the case, at least in those classes whose members can afford to pay for such luxuries. Although the medi- cal profession linq been depleted in both its branches, still i re are sufficient surgeons in London to perf in all the operations that ap- pear to be nc,essary. Nor is there lack of nurses. Perhaps the vogue for applying the surgeon's knife so freely may be killed by the war.

HOME NOTES.

Brown bread used in place of white, will help the food value of a meal.

The steaming of vegetables, instead of boiling, will prevent the most valuable part of these foods being lost. If boiling be preferred, the water in which they have been boiled should be utilised for soup stock, just in the same way as meat stock is used.

Wholemeal makes more nourishing puddings, cakes, sauces, than does white flour, the cost is the same, or less. Potatoes should always be boiled, baked, or steamed in the skins. 'Buttermilk is a cheap and valuable food. A pennyworth of buttermilk, served with pota- toes, is a cheap and wholesome dish. Do not lick stamps or the flaps of envelopes, for the mucilage is made of " any old thing" in the way of glue or gelatine. 3toisten them with your wet finger, or with a bit of dampened cloth or sponge. When bottling fruit or catsup, boil the corks in water for fifteen minutes, or until they are soft and pliable; then they may be driven into the necks of the bottles so tightly that sealing- wax will not be needed.

Run no risks with a stovepipe that has been eaten through with rust. A few pence will get a new pipe, but it would be expensive to build a new house.

Macaroni Cheese.

(1) Parboil for ten minutes in fast boiling and salted water lib. of any kind of macaroni. Drain it well, and put into a saucepan with a little fresh butter, some milk, and plenty of grated Parmesan cheese and some pepper. Simmer until the macaroni is cooked to your taste, turn it out on a hot dish, sprinkle grated Parmesan over, brown with a salamander, and serve. (2) Boil 2oz. of macaroni in water, drain it well; put into a saucepan loz. of butter, mix it well with one tablespoonful of flour, moisten with four tablespoonfuls of veal stock and a gill of cream, add 2oz. of grated Parmesan, one tablespoonful of mustard, salt and cayenne to taste; put in the macaroni, and serve as soon as it is well mixed with the sauce and quite hot.

AppJe Pie. Tare, core and slice thin, ripe, tart apples. Line a pie-dish one or two inches deep with crust, and fill with apples. Mix one tablespoon- ful of flour with four tablespoonfuls of sugar and one-half teaspoonful of grated nutmeg. Sprinkle this over the tops of the apples and add two tablespoonfuls of cold water to the pie. Roll out the upper crust as thin as can be lifted from the table, make a few cuts in it so that the steam can escape, wet the edge of the lower crust; press together, and cut away overhang- ing portions, place in the oven, and bake until a light brown or from fifteen to twenty min-

A good polish for the nails and especially if they are stained is a little lemon juice. Pumice stone in a very fine powder makes a good polish to apply with a piece of chamois skin. To pre- vent the nails from splitting keep them cut short; do not scrape or file them; and moisten with a little glycerine or vaseline.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,— As I was walking through the Park the other day I came across a little boy who was stamping his foot on the ground, and saying to his mother, "I won't h I won't !" I could see that his mother was very angry with him, because she had told him to do some- thing and he had refused. If he happened to be one of my nephews I would be ashamed of him. It is only the naughty children who don't do as they are told. Although the boy lam talking about looked a nice little chap, no one could really like him, because he was not obedient. What does obedience mean? It means that we must do everything father and mother and teacher and everybody older than ourselves tell us to do. But that is not all. It also means that we must do those things willingly and without grumbling. This lesson of obedience has to be learned by everybody, and if you master it while you are young it will save you a lot of trouble who; you leave your school and begin earning wages. All our gallant soldiers and sailors have to be Obedient. They have to do what their officers tell them. in 4zu,workshop anci the factory the workPeoPlo hate to 3 What the foremmi and manators say. The clergymen have to do what the bishop says, and so on. Indeed, all through life we have to do what bthers command. If we don't learn to obey we can never learn to command. All this is not only advi for my nephews, but for my nieces also, so that in future, when father and mother and teacher tell you to do something, do it willingly and take a pride in doing it properly. • This is my message this week; I hope you will take it to heart, and remember it. ` Next week I will announce- a new competition a for Christmas. How many of ou are g' gto try to capture the prize? Write and letime know. Your loving yc UNCLE NED,

Rough Skins. Don't wash your hands and face immediately before going out; it is better for the skin in every way to wash it at least half an hour before going into the air, especially in the winter. Then roughness and redness will be kept away. The girl who will persist in: walking straight into a hot room and standing close by the fire "to warm herself" after coming in from a brisk winter walk, has only herself to thank if she discovers before very long that her nose has become a permanent red. I know more than one girl who suffers from a red nose, and in each case it has been brought on through having a "real good warm" the moment they came in out of the cold. Far better is it to keep in a cooler room for a short while and gradually become used to the warmer air.—" Maud" in "Farm and Home."

No _I magination.

There is a certain business man, ot a tather waggish disposition, who contends that his wife has no imagination. At dinner one night he chanced to mention a tragic circumstance he had read in the evening paper on his way home. A passenger, on a transatlantic steamer had fallen overboard in mid-ocean and had never been seen again. "Was he drowned?" asked his wife.

"Of course not," answered the irrepressible hubby; "but he sprained his ankle, I believe."

The Next Best Thing. She was a widow and he a bluff sailor, who thought the world of her, but not finding it easy to make a landsman' direct proposal, he de- cided to address her in de speech of the sea. " Kate," said he, "your boat is drifting down the stream of life, with no strong hand to steer it safely past the rocks. May Ibe your captain and sail it for you?" .

"No, Jack," Kate answered, with an engag- ing blush, although in a firm tone, "but you may be my second mate if you like."

BIRMINGHAM PENNIES.

Examine the next 1912 penny that you get, and if you see a small "H" immediately before the date you will know that it was made in Birmingham, and is one of the rare coins which circulate in England that have not been made at the Royal Mint in London. You will ask "Why had they to make coins in Birmingham?" Well the answer is easily given. In that year there was so great a de- mand for copper coins that the Mint could not make them all, and had to have a number made at a Mint in Birmingham. Besides the tiny "H" that is stamped on the Bit mingham pennies there is another difference from the ordinary penny. It is thicker, in fact, some are so thick that they won't go into the penny-in-the-slot machines or into the gap meters. There is another question you are likely to flak, and that is, "Why did they stamp the letter "II" on, when Birmingham commences with a ,IThe answer to this is that the place where ese Pennies were made used to be called Hlteait°ll. iEnutevroepsetiang oto o r.ote that o a s s Mint" and "II" is the old initial. smaller had their coins made in the same daCer somecioofn 1

HANDKERCHIEFS. "Where were handkerchiefs first made?" This is a question I have been asked this week, and I think it will be of interest to my nephews and nieces if I give the answer here. It will come as a surprise to you to learn that they were first made at a place called Choi-12'c

in France, where every year they celebrate a pretty custom—that of electing the Handker- chief Queen. The event is very much like our May Day celebrations. The queen, however, is chosen by popularity, and her reign lasts for twelve months, when she is succeeded by an- other.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
Smart Suede Beets.

The superiority of British-made boots and shoes for outdoor use over imported goods has long been acknowledged, but it is not generally known that in the matter of evening footwear I Britain may also claim supremacy. Many people imagine that the dainty, highly finished evening shoes they buy are made ,in Paris or Vienna. This is not always the case. For some years British manufacturers have successfully competed with those on the Continent, and the result is that although supplies have now failed, there is quite as good a choice in the shoo as before the war. Furthermore, thous- ands of women and girls are employed in the making of this class of goods, and should there be any falling off in the demand many will suffer loss of work. With the very short skirts that are to be generally worn for walking smart boots will be required, and already black suede high-buttoned goods have made their appear- ance. They are intensely black, and the buttons are dull also. For country wear brown suede will be very much in evidence. Both black and brown boots have round toes, and practical, sensible heels.

The Picturesque Hat.

• ••- • •---- --- The sailor hat' in its original formiwas very hard and trying to any but the most useful faces. 'lt has undergone many changes since its first appearance, mostly for the better, but its latest developments exceed all others" in grace and charm. It is quite picturesque and is at its best in black velvet and taffetas; the brim is rather wide, but the lines are graceful. Velvet hats are trimmed chiefly with feathers, and black is, seen more frequently than white. The larger hats are steadily coming to the front, but the small ones will not be easily driven from the secure place they have held so long. ArnotT new shapes the soldier's field cap is a prominent favourite. It fits closely to the head, is bound with black braid, and is worn a little on one side.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

Hints for Night Nursing. When sitting up with anyone who is sick, before settling him have everything required for the night in the room. Be sure that the patient is comfortable and warm. Make the fire up. Shade the light with brown or coloured paper. Select a chair to sit in that does not .creak. Do not allow the patient to feel that he is being watched. If he is persistently sleep- less a drink of hot milk may be given; or sponging the face and hands with either quite cold or quite hot water, and a little lavender water or eau de Cologne added. has a soothing effect. Where he has had a restless night, wash him and change his shirt, and make the bed in the early hours of the morning. Gloves should be worn when putting on coal. Hints on Health and Faddism.

Sydney Smith, in his humorous way, pre- tended to compute that between his seventh and seventieth year he had eaten and drunk 44 wag- gon loads more than was good for him; but Theodore Parker, whose constitution was prem- aturely broken down, was serious enough when, not long before he died, he said to a friend, "If twelve years ago I had bought a saddle-horse, or taken real repose in the summer time, I should not have come to this now." I suppose the late Mr. Joseph Chamberlain might truly have said the same. I used to live in the neighbourhood of the late Sir Isaac Holden's residence. He was a fruitarian—with, of course, abundant means for ensuring variety in his diet—and when well advanced in years he made a point of walking at least four miles daily. As to that a well-known minister wrote some time ago: " One honoured friend of mine eats nothing but a single meal a day and walks eight miles after- wards. It is a dreary life! The breakfast-bell brings no joy to him. The call to supper drives him to his lonely room. No afternoon tea, with its delightful gossip, entices him. No supper rare-bit furnishes a pillow for his head. And then that eight miles' walk! In tropical heat or winter storms, up and down the streets, or to and fro from stem to stern of steamers— always eight miles !" Ah, these faddists! Faddism causes some well-to-do people regularly to leave their com- fortable beds at an untimely hour for no good reason whatever, to deny themselves sufficient clothing asleep or during their waking hours, to refuse to close their windows beyond a cer- tain point however severe the weather and the consequent discomfort, and so forth and so on. In this regard there is a great difference between our own people and the southerners. The latter go in for bedroom fires and hot-water bottles; we seem to consider such indulgences almost criminal except in cases of illness. At the same time there are in our midst individuals to whom food fads especially seem to be even more in- teresting than the latest news from the seat of war.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
BRAVE BELGIAN BOY.

We all IoNTe-tO-h-ea;-tale";.‘3l-13;-vet:y, and that I is why I am giving you the story of a brave Belgian boy, who distinguished himself at Liege during the early fighting in this terrible war. The story was told to a reporter working on a newspaper in Paris by a doctor. During the defence of Liege, in which the Belgian army won immortal glory, Corporal Van der Bern, a boy of seventeen, was in com- mand of twenty men who formed a scouting party outside Liege, when suddenly they met a party of fifty Wane. Der Bern's companions turned to retreat, but he wouldn't have that.

"At them!" he cried, then rushed forward boldly. His wonderful bravery bucked up the entire corps, who rushed upon the foe, reckless of the fact, that the odds were overwhelming. One by one the brave men fell, until at last Der Bern and two companions were the sole survivors. They fought as men possessed, but ill its than thirty eecende the two fell, and Der Bern alone remained. Then, and not till then, he realised that all wag lost. He could not leave his two wounded comrades there to die, and he would not sur- render them to the foe. So he seized them in his arms and dragged them to a place of safety. Yet, even as he fled, two German bullets pierced him. He did not falter; he placed his comrades in the care of the Red Cross officers, then stole away to report to his superior officer. Then he fell; but on the following morning General Bertrand, in the presence of all the soldiers, told the story of Van der Bern's magni- ficent courage, and on the spot promoted him to the rank of sergeant.

THE BIRDS DEPART. (BY JESSIE Sum.) Brave bird! speed not on eager wing Across the foam; For we shall miss thy carolling, Who stay at home. And is our joy so brief a thing That thou may'st roam?

" To realms of warmth and light I go." Thy message clear Floats down; and leaves us sad below, As yester-year All, too, was sad. Small joy we know; The clays are drear.

Yet wintry clouds will break in rain, That summer skies May free the brooding earth from pain, Before our eyes. Then shall we hear thy song again, In glad surprise.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 14, 1914

LOCAL PRESBYTERIANISM

St. John's Congregation Keep 60th

Anniversary. EX-MODERATOR GIBSON'S VISIT.

The 60th birthday celebrations of the latest chapter of Presbyterianism in Warrinon were commenced in St. John's Presbyterian Church, Wilderspool-causeway, on Sunday, and will be continued to-morrow and next Sunday. It is just sixty years ago that the congregation was received within the English Presbyterian Synod, but so far back as the days of the Civil War and the Commonwealth Presbyterianism was strong in Warrington. Many trying times have been passed through, and it is pleasing to note that the cause is now enjoying merited pros- perity. Indeed, "extension" is ever the cry. The congregation has set itself to raise £5OO to secure the freehold of the site of the present church, and a further large amount for the securing of additional land and the erection of a hall and class-rooms, the lack of which is hampering the activities of the denomination. As a result of the bazaar held in February about £6OO has been realised, and it is hopefully anticipated that, as a result of the "Diamond Jubilee" offerings and the proceeds of the sale of a very interesting booklet whieh links up the Presbyterianism of to-day with that which was in existence in the Cromwellian and Stuart times, a further £6O will be added. Judging by the large congregations which assembled on Sunday, these expectations will be realised. RENEWED PROSPERITY AFTER DAYS OP TRIAL.

The special preacher: for day on Sunday was the Very Rev. Ex-Moderator Dr. edunro Gibson, whose sermons were full of inspiration and en- couragement. Dr. Gibson, who entered upon his first charge fifty years ago, preached on the words "The Gospel of the Kingdom of God" at the morning service. Referring to the cele- brations he said that as an old friend of the congregation he rejoiced in its renewed pros- perity after the days of trial and losses which had intervened since his last visit. He con- gratulated the minister (the Rev. W. Reid) upon the noble work he had accomplished, and he joined with them most earnestly in the prayer alit the years to come would be better than any that had gone. They all hoped that the second half-century in the history of the revived church would be infinitely better than the first. Much as they loved their Church and appre- ciated the principles of Presbyterianism, it was not in them that they specially glorified, but in the common heritage of all the Churches—the Gospel of the Lord and Saviour. The Presby- terian Church throughout all the world had been characterised for its faithfulness to the Gospel of God, and to the great truths of the Incarnation, the Atonement, the Resurrection, and the full and final Redemption.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
The Gospel and the War.

Speaking on the relation of the Gospel of God to the war, Dr. Gibson said that the Gospel was one of righteousness, peace and joy, whilst the Kaiser's gospel was one of unrighteousness, war and misery. The Kaiser believed that might was right. Righteousness was abolished in favour of might. Ours was the gospel of peace; theirs was the gospel of war. Bern- hardi's book was a glorification of war. The German soldiers carried the book about like a Bible in their knapsacks. The book said: "Blessed are, the war-makers, because they shall be called the children of Odin, who 'is greater than God." That was a devil's doctrine. When righteousness and peace were both re- versed there could be no joy. They had de- plorable proof of that fact in Belgium and Northern France, where the Germans had hacked their way through. The German gospel was that of the kingdom of Satan. The pre- sent was, indeed, a holy war, and ultimately righteousness and peace would triumph against that which was the direct antithesis of these two things. They could rightly say that the Lord of Hosts was with them and that they would prevail. The choir rendered the anthem "0 Lord, my. God," in a tasteful fashion, and the congrega- tion joined in the singing of a jubilee hymn, "God of our fathers, Thee we praise," specially composed by the pastor for the celebrations.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN ANTI-CHRIST DOCTRINES.

In the evening Dr. Gibson took for his text: "For we wrestle not against flesh and blood, but against principalities, against powers, against the rulers of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness in high places" (Ephesians vi. 12). The primary lesson of that passage, said the preacher, had to do with the personal struggle every Christian must main- tain to hold his ground against temptation. Were they quit) sure that they had Christ's high ideal of life before them, and were they trying continuously to live up to it? At some time or other they would have to stand, with all the force they could muster, against an in- visible foe. It might be only one sin to which they were specially tempted and that might appear to be a very small matter. If it was their besetting sin, however small it seemed to be, they must conquer it or lose the battle of life. They must put on all the armour of Christ and stand against temptation. There was, however, a larger conflict than the per- sonal one. The Present World-Conflict. There was a world-conflict going on at the present time, and it was about that and the attitude of Christian people towards it that he would speak. Could they say of it: "We wrestle not against flesh and blood, but against principalities wickedness in high places"? There was that terrible clash of arms. There were millions of their flesh and blood against whom they were fighting to the death. They had no quarrel with the poor German and Austrian soldiers, who were driven like sheep to the slaughter. They were sorry for them. It was not against flesh and blood that they were fighting, but against the evil spirits which had been poison- ing the soul of Germany for 40 years; against the spiritual wickedness in high places of Prus- sia. It was a battle not against the people but against the principalities and powers and the spirits of wickedness which were driving the men on. There were many who did not know all that; men who knew nothing about it until after the war began. Since Bernhardi's kipok—the Devil's Bible in Germany—had been translated into English they had learned that the war was only the embodiment of a legion of spirits of evil, applying their frightful work. The English nation had striven for peace, and it only drew the sword at the summons of truth. There was righteousness on the side of England in the present war—righteousness that came from the fact that she had listened to the appeal of Belgium to honest guarantors, as against a dishonest one. This country was armoured with truth and righteousness and her feet were shod with the preparation of the Gospel of Peace. Righteousness was the only way to peace, and if unrighteousness tried to cross their path, through it they must go. It was a holy war to which the country had been summoned, and, therefore, they could take to themselves the shield of faith and the helmet of salvation.

Anti-Christ Doctrines of Neitzsche. The spirit which animated people to self- sacrifice for timtli, righteousness and peace was the spirit of Christ. It was that spirit which was now opposed to the anti-Christ Neitzschean doctrines. After speaking at length on the words: "Praying always with all prayer and supplication in the Spirit," and enlarging on the power of prayer, Dr. Gibson said there must be no talk of revenge, no matter how great the pravocation—" even it be seventy times seven." They were not Huns, nor had they any Atilla for their leader. There would be righteous indignation, but there must not be any spite or personal or national animosity. Indigna- tion F—Yes. Hatred?—No! There must be an end of the War Lords and an end of the piling up of armaments, but there must not be any burning .of innocent towns, nor the firing of any German dwelling—although, perhaps, they did want to see a fire in Krupp's factory. They wanted a new spirit in the land and the end of Kaiserdora. This had been too terrible a thing for Europe to experience a second time. Some people might think it was hard to talk of forgiving enemies at a time like the present. They could pray that the evil spirits would be east out of Germany and that the Kaiser would ' have his conscience roused and his eyes opened, and that he would be brought to repentance for the greatest crime that had ever been com- mitted, and that his poor soul would be saved. It was for the good of Germany that they should pray. If the evil spirits were cast out Ger-

CHURCHES AND THE WAR.

QUAKERS AND WARFARE. The Spirit that Removes the Occasion for Strife.

A public address on " Christianity and War" was given at the Friends' Meeting House, But- termarket-street, on Sunday night, by Mr. Robert J. Long, of Leeds. The speaker said that the Society of Friends was drawn together, in the first place, from all sorts of diverse places. In many cases, they found their people coming from among the forces of the Crown, for in many cases they felt the time came when they had to Jay down their swords, and quit the service of an earthly king. The first statement of this belief had been made by George Fox, who, when asked to join the forces, said: "I have entered into the spirit that takes away the occasion of all wars." That was the beginning of the peace testimony of the Society of Friends. Men like Fox gradually gathered in little groups. The thought had come to each of them in battle: "What if I should kill a man?" sad they left their guns, prepared to take the con- sequences. They wanted the companionship of God, and the spirit of Christ did not lead them into warfare.

God's Kingdom of Peace and Harmony. They were all of them, in their degree, sharers of the divine nature. As they were willing to surrender their own spirits, there came to them the overshadowing canopy of the Great Spirit. Each individual, therefore, had the power of working either in harmony with that Divine Spirit, or against it. God's kingdom was to be one of peace and harmony; the great message of Christianity was that the spiritual was greater than the material. On what did their safety as civilians depend? They were told that a law was of no use, unless it was backed by public opinion. The Police Force, after all, rested upon that opinion; otherwise a policeman would not be so powerful as he was. They had been willing to surrender themselves to them in a way, and the force was a sort of moral one.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
Mankind Knitted in the Divine Love.

There was, 'nevertheless, 07-great--sense of unity over them all; like responded to like; good to good; evil to evil. The kingdom must be founded by its members giving themselves up utterly to the great ideal before them—utter failure, as it seemed at the moment. The pioneers would have to fall, until the spirit entered the hearts of men; yet, following a high ideal, they must win. Their country and and their lives? These were nothing in them- selves. Mankind knitted together in the love of God—that was the great ideal they had to aim, at.

But an ideal perished if they did not try to live up to it, and, with the great spirit of God brooding over them, there was only room for peace, and joy, and love.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
RUSSIA THE LAST WORLD-POWER. Britain to Bow the Knee, Says Christadelphian Lecturer.

The second of an interesting s cries of lectures was delivered in the Christadelphian Meeting Room, Bold-street, on Monday evening. Mr. Joseph P. Jennings occupied the chair, reading as an introduction Isaiah ii., which deals with the prophecy of the coming of Christ's king. dom; with wickedness being the cause of God's forsaking; and with the powerful effects of God's majesty.

Mr. J. W. Smith, of Huddersfield, was the lecturer, and he took for his subject: "Europe after the War, According to Prophecy"; the outline of the lecture being "the coming dis- appearance of Turkey; Britain and Russia the two world-powers; the result of their policy; the doom of the present condition of things; and the return of Jesus, the only hope for the world's salvation."

Man's Arrogance—A Warning! The lecturer said that this was the time for them to endeavour to elucidate the fact that God had something to do to-day, just as He had had in the past, with regard to men and to nations. 'Many men to-day said that God had nothing at all to do with the earth, but that they had become the dominating factor, and that everything was subject to their will; he would, however, remind them of Nebuchad- nezzar's loss of power in the Babylonian Empire (Daniel iv.). The flood was sent because the peoples of the earth had repudiated the works of the Lord; and they were eating and drinking right up to the coming of the flood.

In Deuteronomy viii. Wa;ii;el the exhorta- tion to obedience to the Jews. That was in 1400 8.C., and the prophecies then made were now being manifested. God said He would scatter the Jews if they did not do what He told them, and it came to pass. Also He said: "hear the word of the Lord, 0 ye nations, and declare it in the isles afar off, and say: He that scattered Israel will gather him, and keep him, as a shepherd doth his flock."

The Repatriation of the Jews. With regard to the scattering, 1,700 years expired without a Jew being allowed to live in the land; and to-day there were 100,000 back in Palestine—over 60,000 in Jerusalem alone! The Jews had not gone back to their land for com- mercial reasons, but because they had been per- secuted in- all other lands. Now, however, their commerce was growing rapidly. The Lecturer suggested that Russia was the only independent nation mentioned in the Scriptures; it being the " thou" in Ezekiel xxxviii.: "Thou shalt ascend and come like a storm, thou shalt be like a cloud to cover the land. . . . . It shall also come to pass that thou shalt think an evil thought, and . • • go to them that are at rest, . . . having neither bars nor gates, to take a spoil, and to take a prey."

India; the Tarshish of Prophecy. With Russia thus marching against Palestine, Britain entered. In verse 13 they read that "the merchants of Tarshish, with all the young lions thereof," should rise. Tarshish, the lec- turer showed, was India; and it was to be defeated. Therefore Britain, which it repre- sented, was to bow the knee to Russia, which would be the last world-power. Then the fury of the Lord God would arise—" I will magnify myself; and I will be known in the eyes of many nations, and they shall know that I am the Lord."

===== 0002601_19141114_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
CHRISTMAS MAILS NOTICE.

The Postmaster-General gives notice that, in view of the war, it is not practicable to give previous notice of the dates and routes of the transmission of mails for places abroad; and in these circumstances the usual list of dates for posting for Christmas and New Year mails for places abroad will not be issued this year. It is particularly requested that letters and par- cels intended for arrival about (1) Christmas Day, or (2) New Year's Day, should be posted early, as under existing conditions all foreign and colonial mail services are liable to delay and to interruption without notice.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL PRESBYTERIANISM—continued.

many would enjoy a new era of light, peace and joy. What Belgium Has Not Lost. There was the inspiring vision of a better world behind the storm and carnage of war. This country was on the side of the Angels against the Spirits of Night, and above the din of battle they might hear the angel-song—almost silent as yet; but it was going to sound out louder in the future—" Glory to God in the highest, and on the earth peace, good-will to men."

The Germans had been boasting for many years of "The Day," meaning the day of Ger- many's victor and Britain's downfall. They, as Britons, would say, on the other hand, "Here's to the day when God shall come !"

Some people said the Belgians had lost every- thing, but they had not, for their honour still remained. Even if what they had lost was to be permanent, it would be nothing to what their oppressor had lost when-he began by losing his lionour. That was far more than all the Bel- gians had lost. They should all strive their hardest in the individual struggle and conquer their besetting sins, so that the "God of battles would give them the ultimate victory." The anthem "The Lord is my Shepherd" was pleasingly rendered by the choir.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
CLERGY ON RR AND CREEDS.

Home Rule and Welsh Acts Condemned.

"AFTER THE WAR."

The annual conference in connection. with the Rural Deanery of Winwick was held in the Church House, Warrington, on Wednesday, the Rural Dean (the Rev: Canon Willis) presiding over a chapter of the clergy in the afternoon, at which it was decided to record on the min- utes the fact that the Revs. W. Bracecamp and H. F. S. Collier have gone to the front to serve as chaplains to the Forces. Papers on "The Importance of the Creeds, and their Interpreta- tion," were read by the Rev. Canon Lancelot, principal of Liverpool College, and the Rev. R. F. Herring, of Toxteth Park, Liverpool. In the course of his paper, Canon Lancelot said creeds were important to him, for they safeguarded the trustworthiness of Christ, in so far as human language could. The Rev. R. F. Herring said creeds were sum- maries of a faith already attained. They were not for those "outside," but for those "inside." They marked the parting of the ways between dogmatic and undogmatic religions, and, there- fore, between the Catholic religion and the Protestant religion. They were the Church's protest against those who denied the miraculous in Christianity. The. Church in these days was challenged to re-assert her mind, and this was no time in which to curtail the use of any of the creeds.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S MORAL BREAKDOWN.

In the course of the discussion which ensued, the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton said that they had had wonderful experience of the complete break- down of what Christianity there was in Ger- many and the nature of it. There was an opportunity for them now to bring the import- ance of the Christian faith before the people, and show what had been the result of a com- plete breakdown of the moral fibre and character of the German nation.

Canon Stevenson thought the only difficulty with which they were face to face was that they were all agreed. (Laughter.) The Rev. B. Percival said that the working classes were turning to the faith more than they had done. The Rev. C. E. Hill said the present exhibi- tion of German barbarism spoke volumes against the so-called Christianity of the great German Empire. He added that he believed that the creeds should be said in a reverent manner without the organ accompaniment, and nbt rattled off as they were in many churches. _ _ _ _

The Rev. G. Vale Owen aid the Rev. W. Pollock-Hill also joined in the discussion.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
IS HOME RULE OPPORTUNE?

A conference of laity and clergy was held in the evening, when papers were given on "The duty of patriotic Churchmen to the Church in Wales after the war" and "The duty of patriotic Churchmen to Ireland after the war," the former by Mr. C. Stidston-Broadbent, the secretary of the Central Church Committee, and the latter by the Rev. Canon Rountree, rector of Stretford, Manchester. Canon Rountree said it was questioitable, apart from the morality of the Home Rule ques- tion itself, whether it was opportune or fair to force an Act through at the present time, hav- ing regard, to the fact that it involved a great constitutional crisis, and possibly, even, a revo- lution, and also bearing in mind that the nation was face to face with a great national peril. Would Home Rule be innocuous; would it pro- mote the happiness and prosperity of Ireland; would it make for peace? Or, on the other hand, would it increase religious strife, para- lyse industry and destroy public confidence? Could these questions be answered? Mr. Red- mond produced quite a dramatic effect by his celebrated declaration at the outbreak of the war, but the " Times " stated that "Mr. Red- mond's campaign has so far proved a disappoint- ment. At the most liberal estimate it would appear that not more than 10,000 recruits have come forward in answer to his appeal, and that these include a strong contingent from Dublin and Cork, where special conditions prevail." It was evident that Mr. Redmond could not be taken as the mouthpiece of the Dome Rule party, and that he could not say what his supposed followers would or would not do. He was inclined to think it would be the duty of those who sympathised with the Unionists of Ireland, and more particularly with their fellow Churchmen in Ireland, to insiSt, when the first opportunity occurred after the ,war, that the Home Rule Act be repealed, if it were not en- dorsed by the people on the occasion of a general election.

AN EFFECT THE WAR MAY HAVE. The Rural Dean said they hoped one result of the lamentable war would be to bring them closer together. When Irishmen from Ulster and other places had been fighting, side by side, he hoped they would not want to fight between themselves when they came back. The Rev. E. C. E. Carleton did not think they could do very much with regard to what would happen after the war. They were in the hands of very good lenders—of course, he was speaking of the Unionist party. The Home Rule battle would be largely a political one, and could be left safely in the hands of the political leaders.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
GOVERNMENT WILL "GO BACK."

Mr. Stidston-Broadbent dealt briefly with his subject, and at the close expressed the .opinion that the present Government would go back to power in November next. They must, he said, bring in a strong agitation for the repeal of the present Act.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
AN UNCOMPROMISING RESOLUTION.

After a discussion, in which the Revs. A. White, E. C. E. Carleton, B. H. Watts and A. M. Mitchell took part, the following resolution was carried : " That the refusal of the Government to sus- pend the Established Church (Wales) Act or to postpone its operation until the end of the war takes an unfair advantage of the loyalty of Churchmen and adds greatly to their anxieties and. responsibilities at a time when they are desirous of devoting their whole energy and resources to a united national policy. (2) Fur- ther, such action will compel an increased de- sire to agitate for a repeal of the Act immedi- ately the end of the war is in sight. (3) The Conference desires to convey to the bishops and Churchpeople in Wales the deepest sympathy with them in the cruel position in which they are placed by the passing of the Act, and to assure them that it recognises its duty to' assist them: in every possible way." , Mr. J. H. Smethurst was re-elected treasurer. and the Rev. G. Vale Owen secretary.

RECRUITING AND LABOUR.

Recruiting for Lord Kitchener's Army and the mobilisation of the Reserves and Territorials has withdrawn a certain amount of labour from farms. In the South of England, at any rate, I think the skilled labourer is remaining. Farmers, I know, have a habit of trusting casual labourers to call for a job, but in these special times of war it is necessary to have some exchange for employer and employed. In some districts voluntary agencies have been started with success. The Labour Exchanges have made most strenuous efforts, and have made quite new regulations with regard to the supply of farm hands. Labour Exchanges for some reason are not popular with farmers, but 1 ven- ture to hope that any farmers requiring new hands will at least call at their nearest exchange and interview the manager. They will find him helpful and courteous, and it may lead to busi- ness being done.—Mr. E. T. Eve, before the London Farmers' Club.

COMMITTEE.

Town Council Debate a Question of Principle.

SQUABBLE ABOUT DELEGATES & COMMITTEEMEN.

'rhe nembers of the Warrington Town Conn- -11 devoted nearly three hours on Monday after- , to appointing their representatives on . arious other public bodies and to discussing lii;°Plased alterations in their Standing Orders, ~ e debate °Mimes approaching the acrimon- "s• The newly re-elected Mayor (Alderman eter Peacock) presided over the deliberations, _ d the other members of the Council present were ; The Deputy - Mayor (Councillor ,c)seph), Aldermen Bolton, Tinnion, James •r•A'ans, Pierpoint, Smethurst, Bennett, and John , vans ; and Councillors McKee, Fox, rakins, Litalley, Dickenson, Gough, Broad- ,B,llrse, Halliwell, Wilkinson, Locker, Henshall, • "aw, Skelland, Barton, Pemberton, Lashford, , unnington, Archer, Chrimes and Chantler ; Logether with the Town Clerk (Mr. • J. Lyon yVltittle). /IL, in. i '"LLEGATES ON COUNTY BODIES.

not find another gentleman in the Council who would fill this representative position on the Asylums Board, or on any other Board? (A Voice: "Rot !")

• Repeated Re-appointment Wrong! The question at issue was a question of prin- ciple, not of individuals. The principle of re- appointing again and again was a principle which was wrong. Alderman Tinnion said that they ha cf no right to say that a man should only hold a certain position for three years. If the Council had not the right to say that, then who had? No man in the. Council respected Alderman Evans more than he (the speaker) did, but in taking the attitude he was on this question he was fighting for a principle; fight- ing to give other members of the Council an opportunity to rise, to show whether they had any abilities or not. DOCTOR OR BAKER BEST—WHICH? Councillor PEMBERTON also supported Coun- cillor Archer, remarking that it was very un- fortunate that, names should have been intro- duced; in fact, it was quite improper. The Standing Order under discussion was made to apply to. the Inebriates' Acts Board, the Asylums Board, the County Rate .Toint Com- mittee, the Lancashire and Western Sea Fish- eries Board, and the Old. Age Pensions Com- mittee. Yet, for some reason or other, Alder- man Tinnion had merely singled out one ap- pointment. Why? It was very nice and proper that Alderman Tinnion should get up and say nice things about his life-long friend, Alder- man James Evans, but, as well as his duty to his friend, he had a duty to the Council and to the public outside, and there was no earthly reason at all why the Council should be forced to accept Alderman Tinnion's valuation of Alder- man James Evans. He (the speaker) was sorry to have to be personal, but he was going to talk very straight. It did not take a very clever man to know that asylums were institu- tions for dealing with the mentally deficient. One, therefore, would think that if the Council were desirous of appointing the best man to represent them on a body having to do with that class of person they would not employ a baker. (Cries of "Oh !Oh !") What ~did he know about it? Now, if they had a doctor —! There were three of them on that Council, but, of course, they knew nothing--or, the Council apparently thought they didn't! They were not fit; the best man was a baker! (Laughter.) He asserted that it was an insult to the doctors. . "These Posts Ought to Go Round." Again, looking at the matter from the admin- istrative side, they had men on the Council who managed large concerns—men who wele con- ducting large businesses—and yet they said: "These men can't do, and never will be able to do, what Alderman Evans has done !" Those posts on the outside bodies, the speaker went on to contend, ought to go round as a matter of course. He realised that it was a very painful thing to have to take a stand like this, and he also realised that on an issue of the kind there were a .lot of gentlemen who did not like voting by a show of hands, and, personally, he thought they ought to have a ballot. (Hear, hear.) They only elected the Mayor for one year. Was he not an important official? He was; then why should they go on electing these lesser officials year after year? He was begin- ning to think that those appointments must be very, very valuable, else why all the great effort that was made to hold them? He was going to dig very deeply into that matter; he was going to inquire into it.i (Laughter.) When people held so tenaciously to these posts there must he something in it. Fancy the impertinence of Alderman Tinnion in saying that the Council had no right to alter the existing practice of making these appointments.

Voting By Ballot Suggested. How did the Council know that they could not get a better man until they had tried? (Cries of " Vote !") The Port Sanitary Auth- ority had been mentioned. There were two or three doctors on the Council—men whose busi- ness it was to deal with sanitary affairs and disease—and yet they were being told that the only man who could deal with those matters was the Chairman of the Health Committee, who was a baker! Fancy a man telling him that he did not know how to deal with a build- ing job, and that the only man who did was a baker! (Laughter.) Fancy those men who had studied, those men who had paid thousands of pounds to qualify for their profession, being told that a baker was a better man for the job than a doctor! He was prepared to move a Standing Order that in future they voted on these questions by ballot. As it was, there was too much canvassing. (Hear, hear.) SECOND LINE OF ATTACK. Is There Remuneration?

Councillor ARCHER: The enemy is not finally repelled. We can come again! In fact, I am coming again now, and I move that no member of the Council be appointed to sit on more than one of the public bodies on which the Council have a right to appoint representatives; 'instead of two, as at present. Councillor LASHFORD seconded. Councillor GOUGH supported, remarking that those offices carried a certain amount of re- muneration, and he did not think one or two members only ought to keep on getting that remuneration. It was contrary to the very principle of constitutional government; it was contrary to all human expectation. (Laughter.) The same men ought not to get those positions everlastlligly; it was ridiculous on the face of , it. ' The MAYOR said that only out-of-pocket ex- penses were paid. Alderman TINNION : This is another attempt by unfair means—not straightforward means— to carry a point that you could not carry if you fought it on personal lines. Councillor PEMBERTON : That remark is the worst you have made this afternoon. It is not at all unfair to attempt to alter the Standing Orders—this is the time and place to do so; the only time of the year it can be done without special resolution. Councillor DICKENSON : Some people seem to think that there is remuneration attaching to some of these offices. The MAYOR : Surely you can understand a clear answer to a clear question.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 14, 1914.

• Councillor DICKENSON : I think the Town Clerk ought to give us it very straight. , The MAYOR: I tell you that only out-of-pocket expenses are paid. What more do you want? This amendment was also defeated.

that the name of Councillor Chrimes figured in the list read out. He had spoken to Councillor Chrimes upon what he was about to say. Councillor PEMBERTON : I rise to a point of order. The constitution of the committee is decided now.

Alderman Tismox Very well, I will move an amendment.

The MAYOR said the Alderman would be quite in order in doing so.

ALDERMAN TINNION'S "PAINFUL DUTY."

Alderman TxxxxoN, continuing, said that he felt it to be his painful duty to oppose the election of Councillor Chrimes to that commit- tee. The Councillor and he were friends, so there could be no question of a personal char- acter upon the course he was taking. It was simply a matter of principle. The Watch Com- mittee was the body that governed the police of the town and the police very frequently had to deal with licence-holders. Councillor Chrimes's personal character was such that he (Alderman Tinnion) could not take the slightest exception, to him on that ground, but he was a licensee, and, as such, he was bound to be brought into contact with the police. The police were only human, and how could they dissociate in their minds the fact that Coun- cillor Chrimes, the licence-holder, was also Councillor Chrimes, one of their masters? He could not help feeling, and saying, that Coun- cillor Chrimes, being a licensee, had no right to sit on the Watch Committee. They should not have a gentleman who was a licensee and who had to observe the licensing laws on the com- mittee which governed the police, who had to see that those laws were carried out. Of course there was the case of Alderman Bolton, but he kthe speaker) was told that that gentleman was not a licensee, and, besides, he got on the com- mittee was he was Mayor of the borough—he got on as chief magistrate, when there is no alternative. His opinion was that they should not have one gentleman connected with the Trade on the committee, whilst to have two— well, it would not be fair either to the police, or to the Chief Constable. He wished that, having regard to all the circumstances, Coun- cillor Chrimes would see his way clear to with- draw his name, for his presence on the commit- tee would place his fellow-members in a rather invidious position. He moved that the name of Councillor Chrimes be deleted from the list and that of Councillor Craik substituted. Councillor BARTON seconded. Councillor CHANTLER asked whether there was any law which prevented a licence-bolder sitting on a Watch Committee., The MAYOR replied in the negative. Councillor ARCHER said he as surprised at Alderman Tinnion's speech. Councillor Chrimes had done signal service for the friendly societies and philanthropic institutions of the town.

Alderman Tpanon : I grant that. Councillor ARCHER: I appraise the qualities of Councillor Chrimes as highly as you do. He has done good in the town, and nobody can point the finger of suspicion at him. The speech we have just heard is an insult to him and to the intelligence of the town. I do not think anybody will adjudicate on any committee in a fairer or more broad-minded manner than will Councillor Chrinies, be it on the Watch Committee or any other committee. (Hear, hear.) The argument which has been brought to bear against Councillor Chrimes might be used against other members of the committee— those who possess narrow views on the opposite side. If those men were entitled to sit on the Watch Committee, and deal with the licensed trade, so was Councillor Chrimes. (Hear, hear.) A more insulting speech against an individual who conducts his business on fair, broad-minded lines was never delivered by an Alderman of this town than the one we have just listened to. (Hear, hear.) Councillor CHRIMES : I thank you for your pluck!

COUNCILLOR CHRIMES A "FIT AND PROPER PERSON."

Councillor Baomnitlasi' pointed out that both in committee and in open Council the members had passed resolutions in favour of Councillor Chrimes being on the Watch Committee. Did Alderman Tinnion think that the perception of the Councillors was of such small calibre that they did not perceive the position he had outlined when they voted for Councillor Chrimes? The strongest reason why he supported Councillor Chrimes was that during his (the speaker's) election campaign he was asked by the Tem- perance party whether; if elected a member of the Corporation, he would vote-against apy per- son who was interested, directly or indirectly, in the liquor traffic, being placed on the Watch Committee or on the Bench, and he replied that where his conscience dictated to him that any member of the Corporation was a fit and proper person to sit on any committee, whether he was interested either directly or indirectly in the Trade—either as a publican or the holder of an outdoor licence—he should vote for that man. What was the result? He gained a 346 majority on that very question—the greatest majority he had scored in any election yet, and he had fought four, all on questions of that description. For many years Councillor Chrimes had been secretary of a trade union, which showed that a large number of people had every confidence in him. They must not run away with the idea that he (the speaker) held a brief for the liquor trade, for he had never tasted beer since he was seven years old, and he considered that he was as temperate as any men on the Council. (A Voice: "More shame for you to say it 1". and laughter.) That Councillor Chrimes was not biased or a fanatic was proved by his work for the Friendly Societies, and the confidence that , large bodies of men placed in him. (Hear, hoar ) NOT AN ORDINARY LICENCE-HOLDER. Councillor PEMBERTON said he MILS sorry that Alderman Tinnion should have made a personal attack on Councillor Chrimes. Alderman Bol- ton was on the comthittee, and he had to do not only with one licensed house, but with many such houses. Councillor Chrimes was not a licence-holder in the ordinary sense. He was a legitimate grocer, and sold groceries, and only held an off-beer licence in connection with his 'imain blisiness. He donsidered that the attack which had been made on the worthy Councillor was most uncalled for—(hear, hear) —in fact, it was painful and improper, as well as uncalled for. He (the speaker) was sorry and surprised that Alderman Tinnion had so little confidence in the Chief Constable. What He Would Not Ask The Chief!

lie, personally, should be sorry to be the one to attempt to get the Chief to do something for either himself or a friend that it was not his (the Chief's) duty to do, and, in that connec- tion, he would like to zay that he did not ever remember coming across a gentleman who had carried out the duties of his high office with more integrity , and straightforwardness than had the Chief. Then what about the other members of the committee? Did Alderman Tinnion mean to say that Councillor Chrimes was going to dominate over , the other eleven? Were they all going to get under the table when he barked? (Laughtdil The law allowed licence-holders to be on Watch Committees. There was not a man in the town who had done more for his fellows than had Councillor Chrimes, and he was respected and looked up to by thousands of men in the town as one whose integrity and honesty were above reproach. Yet he had to 'come there that day and stand odious insinuations as to what he was going to do—in the minds of some people. He was not going to get £4O or .:,t5O a year in travelling ex- penses. (Laughter.) None of those little bits to pick up would come his way. It was abomin- able that such things should be said about a gentleman. IF TEETOTALERS, WHY NOT A LICENSEE? Alderman BOLTON failed to see anything un- reasonable in putting Councillor Chrimes on the Watch Committee, and he could not see that the teetotalers had any cause for fear. There , were more teetotalers on that committee than there were men of "any other denomination." (Laughter.) Councillor SHAW said he was sorry that, in spite of the friendship they enjoyed in Friendly Society work, he would, on principle, have to vote against Councillor Chrimes going on the Watch Committee. - Councillor LASHFORD wanted to know why the Council were "going to make fish of one and flesh of another?" If they applied the principle that Alderman Tinnion had talked about to the Watch Committee, why not apply it to the other committees also? They would not have him on the Health Committee six years ago because he was a butcher. It was very unfair for Alderman Tinnion to have got up as he had done and "scandalise" Councillor Chrimes, who was one of the most fit and proper persons in the Council to be on the Watch Committee. (Hear, hear.) Alderman TINNION 'said he knew nothing per-

sonally against Councillor Chrimes, and denied that he had the slightest intention of making a scandalous, or shy other kind of attack, upon him.

The amendment was lost, and the nesult 131 the ballot approved.

lOW MANY ALDERMEN TO A

COMMITTEE?

Alderman TINNION then moved that Standing

Order 34—" iia-.m--,;re-illa-n—four Aldermen shall be appointed upon any committee "—be deleted. Supposing a committee already had four Alder- men upon it, and then a fifth member, who perhaps might be the most useful of all the members, was elevated to the Aldermanic Bench, what would happen? Who would have to give way and come off? Were the Council going to say that Aldermen were not as capable of managing committee work as other people. Personally he had met a great nany ratepayers, who, although he was an Alderman, thanked him for the very intelligent and fearless way in which he represented them. Other people represented the ratepayers besides Councillors, and many of the Councillors who got in ought to remember that they did so thanks to consid- erable help from some of the Aldermen. Alderman BOLTON seconded. If they were to have only four Aldermen on a committee, then, by all the laws of proportion, they ought only to have ten Councillors. If they limited the number of Aldermen they should also limit the number of Councillors; one was as good as the other. (Hear, hear, and some laughter.)

===== 0002601_19141114_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMEN WHO WANT TO PICK THEIR

JOBS.

Councillor ARCHER supported the Standing Order. He complained that the Aldermen wanted to pick and choose the committees on which they should serve, saying, in effect, to the other members of the Council : "You stand by and take what we leave you!" Was that fair to the administration of the town?

Alderman Bourox : Why don't you make a Standing Order that there shall be no Alder- man on any committee? (Laughter.) Councillor BROADHURST said that this griev- ance of the number of Aldermen on the various committees was a grievance under which the Councillors had been labouring for many years, and the time was ripe for wiping the grievance away. The Aldermen were not necessarily the cream of the intelligence of the Corporation. (Laughter.) Alderman Tinnion sounded his own trumpet very well, because nobody else would do it for him. (Renewed laughter.) Fre- quently, he confessed, he admired Alderman Tinnion, but he thought that gentleman was on the wrong tack now. Something had been said about out-of-pocket expenses. There was not much chance of any Councillor getting any out- of-pocket expenses for, say, deputations to Lon- don, when they had Aldermen as chairmen and deputy-chairmen of the committees. They al- ways took jolly good care that they, got the out-of-pocket expenses. (Laughter.)

" UNDER-ALDERMANNED" COMMITTEES. Councillor PEMBERTON said that the law fixed the number of Aldermen at one-fourth of the Council, and he thought that would be a very good principle to apply to committees also. Several committees were under-aldermanned. For instance, the Small Holdings Committee only included two, whilst the Cemetery Com- mittee had none. Again, the Electricity and Gymnasium Committees only had two Alder- men on them. New members coming on to the Council ought not to be stifled; it was an insult to put them on to committees like the Cemetery Committee as a sort of make-weight. Councillor GOUGH said his experience of Alder- men was that they tried to get the best positions on the Council. Alderman Tinnion was asked to go on the Cemetery Committee. He said "Oh, no!" He did not want to go to the Cemetery; he would be there soon enough. (Laughter.) The Aldermen liked to go on the Gas Com- I rnittee, the Sewerage and Paving Committee, and the Water Committee, where there was some spending of money. (Laughter.) He thought the Standing Order a fair one, and he was going to support it. On the Watch Com- mittee there were seven Aldermen ,and ‘five Councillors; how were the Councillors going to carry anything in face of the Aldermen?

Councillor WILKINSON announced his intention of voting for the Standing Order. He never knew the Aldermen to do anything dishonour- able.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEAD-SET AGAINST THE ALDERMEN

Alderman BENNETT said he was sorry to hear that discussion at the present time; he thought they might have deferred their unseemly squabbles for another year. There was a great deal to be said for proportional representation, which, in this instance, would mean one-fourth of the Aldermen to each committee. The same proportion ought to apply all through. That was democracy, but he was not at all sure that it was the best way of getting the best govern- ment. He protested against the dead-set that was being made against the Aldermen. It seemed to be in the minds of some members

that an Alderman was gifted i-vitcetelogie-Ls-e of original sin. (Laughter.) Instead of it being a case of their being proud of their office, it would appear that they ought to humble themselves, and say to themselves that it was case of none being so poor as to do them rever- ence. (Laughter.) The more time they gave to the town, the more they tried with heart and soul to serve it, the more kicks and insults they seemed to get. It was not playing the game, and some of them were getting a little bit fed up with it. It was a little hard that they, after doing their best according to their lights, should be turned on just as if they had some ulterior motive. He could assure the Conncil that he had no ulterior motive; no motive other than that of serving the town. Had he studied his own personal comfort he would have left the Council years ago; in fact, he would never have been in it. But his services, and the ser- vices of the other Aldermen, instead of being appreciated only seemed to merit opprobrium. Again the amendment was defeated. PROPOSAL TO PUNISH, POOR ATTENDEHS.

Councillor WILKINSON proposed as a new Standing Order that no member of the Council, whether Alderman or Councillor, should retain his position on any committee of the Council, if he had not, during the previous twelve months, put in fifty per cent, of possible attendances. He wished to cast no personal reflections, but, for the life of him, he could not see how a member of the Corporation could possibly keep a grasp of the work of the committee to which he belonged if he only put in, say, about three attendances out of thirty. (Hear, hear.) No member should be allowed to stay away from more than half of the meeting i and remain on a committee, unless hezvill, or could, give some other bona-fide reasons. Councillor ARCHER : You will have a lot of bona-fide reasons. Alderman SMETHURST seconded.

Alderman Tmmorr said he did not are how many committees they pulled him off-he was finishing with the whole thing; it was getting disgusting. It was all very well Wr a gentle- man of leisure and means, who had nothing better to do, to try and impose such a regula- tion, as that proposed, On business men, who had so much other work to do besides that of serving the town.

_ Councillor WILKINSON said there was nothing new in his proposal; it operated on the Educa- tion Committee. A VOICE : Twenty-five per cent. Councillor WILKINSON: I will make this 25 per cent., with allowance for illness. If it operates effectively on the Education Committee it will also operate effectively on the other committees. The proposition was lost, Councillor WILKIN- SON remarking: "I am sorry, Mr.' Mayor, that the proposition should have been defeated by the party of Progress." (Laughter.)

===== 0002601_19141114_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
A DOCTOR FOR A DOCTOR'S JOB!

Councillor PEMBERTON said that an appoint- ment of this kind was one for which a medical man was eminently fitted, and how curious the doctors on the Council must think it to find them appointing the men they did, when obvi- ously there were members of the Corporation more suited for the position. It was lowering to the dignity of the medical men to be passed over as they were. Apart from all that, thr honours ought to go round, and should not btr

stuck to in the indecent manner they were. As far as he was concerned, the point was one that he should press to an issue every time. They would not think of putting a boiler-maker to a joiner's job, yet they put laymen in these ap- pointments when they had professional men who were eminently suited to fill the posts at their command, simply because of cliques, whoso primary purpose was to put their friends in. Alderman SMETHURST denied the insinuation that he had ever touted for votes in that Coun- cil, and he thought the last speaker ought to withdraw the suggestion. It was most disgrace- ful that after twenty-four years' service he should be accused of such a thing, and he could assure the members that he had never askeit Tor a single vote outside the Council in his life. Alderman BOLTON said he believed that there was only one drunken woman from Warrington in the Inebriates' Home, and was it suggested that Alderman Smethurst was incapable of look- ing after her interests? Alderman SMETHURST: Anybody who wants the job can have it.

ONE WAY OF SAYING "NO, TI-LANK YOU!" The DEPUTY-MAYOR said he had no sympathy with the amendment, and seconded the original resolution.

Councillor PEMBERTON : Will you refuse to serve?

The DEPTJTY-MAYOR : Certainly, under the pres- ent circumstances.

Councillor PEMBERTON : Then I withdraw my amendment.

Alderman Smethurst was then re-appointed. Alderman TINNION proposed the re-election of Alderman Bennett as the Council's representa- tive on the County Road Joint Committee. Councillor PEMBERTON said he opposed on a matter of principle. He had no personal inter- est in the matter, and he did not want that appointment, or any other, but he protested strongly against the same gentlemen having those appointments year after year, and also against the fact that in a great many eases those appointed were aldermen, whom the public could not remove, but who they had to have whether they wanted or not. He did feel that those honours ought to go round fairly and equitably, and therefore he proposed, as an amendment, that Councillor Archer be gixen the appointment, feeling sure that he would do the work as well as did Alderman Bennett.

Councillor CIINNINGTON seconded the amend- ment. _ _

The amendment was lost, and the proposition carried.

Alderman Pierpoint was re-elected the Coun- cil's representative on the Lancashire and Western Sea Fisheries Board, and Councillor Henry Roberts was again selected as the Coun- cil's nominee on the governing body of the Warrington Training College. Alderman James Evans was re-elected to represent the Council on the Afanchester Port Sanitary Authority, but not without opposition, Councillor PEMBERTON unsuccessfully proposing that the appointment be given to Councillor Joseph, and Councillor LpIiFORD seconding. The Council then rose, having sat from noon until close upon four o'clock.

IRON AND STEEL TRADES' FEDERATION.

Annual Conference.

Owing to thr war the Southport Conference of the Steel Trades' Federation was practically re- duced to an Executive meeting with Conference powers. It is of interest to note that the re- ports showed considerable progress, and that during the year good work had been done. Something like £2,000 had been paid in lock-out benefit, principally in the Scottish area. Mr. James Gavin was again unanimously elected as chairman for the ensuing year, and Mr. George Beadle, as vice-chairman. There are pnly two changes on the Executive Council. The chairman's opening address was character- ised by a cheerful optimism as to the future prospects of the Federation. The condition of trade and the war had naturally been unfavour- able to greater progress.

The question of registering the Federation as a trade union was postponed for a more com- plete revision of the rules. Soldiers' pay and dependents' allowances were discussed, and the secretary (Mr. J. Cox) was instructed to send resolutions to the Premier, Mr. Lloyd George, Mr. McKenna, and Dr. Macnamara, in which the Federation advocate the minimum allowance of £1 per week to dependents of men losing their lives, and also soldiers incapacitated. During the discussion, it was generally urged that there would be no- difficulty in getting men if they were properly paid and their dependents guar- anteed against the horrors of want. Consider- able administrative work was transacre-d; and the Conference broke up on Saturday afternoon.

Every winter one hears the familiar grumble about the English climate, from victims of rheumatism who are more or less disabled by stiffness, and cruel pains in the muscles and joints. But although wintry weather sets rheumatism raging through the system, the trouble in most cases arises in the blood, which has become impure. What is needed is a change of blood.
