DRINK IN WAR TIME.

Earlier Closing of Public-Houses

Advocated

AT BIG TEMPERANCE MEETING

"In view of the social and economic evils inseparably connected with the present facilities for the sale of intoxicating liquors, and in view of the action taken by other European nations in the direction of prohibit- ing the sale of intoxicating liquor at this time, this meeting is of opinion that the Intoxicating Liquors (Temporary Restriction) Act, 1914, is wholly inadequate to meet the circumstances of the time, and urges Parlia- ment to pass a general measure greatly cur- tailing the hours of opening in licensed houses and clubs, and giving to the Licensing Authority in every licensing district full discretionary powers of still further limiting the hours of sale, if, in their judgment, the economic and moral conditions of the neigh- bourhood require it." The foregoing resolution was carried unani- mously at a numerously attended public meet- ing held under the auspices of the Warrington Band of Hope Union in the Co-operative Hair on Sunday night. Mrs. James Dodds was in the chair, and supporting her on the platform were a number of well-known Temperance workers and advocates, including the Revs. T. T. James, A. J. Chessum, Irvine Lister and C. P. Pitt, and Messrs. Robert Watson (of Lan- caster, representing the United Kingdom Alliance), P. Gandy (secretary), and Griffiths.

VICAR OF PADGATE SUPPORTS. The Rev. H. F. S. Collier, of Padgate, wrote regretting his inability to be present, as, having been appointed chaplain to the West Lancashire Division, he had "received orders to flit," and would be gone before the meeting was held. Another time, if spared, he would be glad to help to the best of his ability. The reading of the letter evoked loud applause. DON'T "TREAT" TOMMY ATKINS. Mrs. DODDS, in the course of a brief address, expressed the pleasure it afforded her to once again take the chair as that annual gathering on Temperance Sunday, to once again have the opportunity of standing shoulder to shoulder with the brave raen and women, who, though often- times working under great disabilities and dis- couragements, were, nevertheless, spreading the leaven that was eventually going to leaven a big whole. She did not think there was ever a time in the history of the world when Tem- perance men and women needed to show their colours so much as at present. .It was a terrible thing that, in a time like that through which the country was passing, it was necessary for Lord Kitchener to appeal to the men and women of the country to abstain from giving our soldiers drink. It was terrible that the great General should have had to issue such an intimation, because surely everybody must know that the men who had so bravely and courage- ously joined the Army to fight for their King and country wanted to be as fit as they possibly could be—physically, mentally, morally and spiritually fit—and they could not be fit if they began to drink. It behoved those present that night to do all they could—to do their very utmost—to spread the cause, not so much of temperance—for, after all, temperance was a word that admitted of wide construction—as of teetotalism. (Applause.) RUSSIA'S EXAMPLE TO THE WORLD. The Rev. A. J. Cazssum then moved the resolu- tion quoted above. In doing so he said that if there ever was a time when the question of temperance ought to be a kind of burning im- perative in the Church's programme, it was now, for never was there a time when it was so much needed. Just as they were beginning to feel that it was hard work and slow work in these days to get anything like temperance re- form on a rapid scale., there came a voice—an encouraging voice—from a most unexpected quarter, a most unlikely quarter—from Russia. The last country in the world had spoken with no uncertain sound. Who would have expected Russia to take the decisive stand she had with regard to her Army? She had been crippled by Tsardom and by serfdom, and hampered by 130 different religious sects. Her people—a motley crew—had been kept down in compara- tive ignorance; but now, from her heart, came that decisive voice—the voice of the Tsar—" Our soldiers shall have no strong drink; our spirit shops shall be closed. Although it means a loss of £90,000,000 a year, I will take this stand per- manently." (Applause.) France had, to a cer- tain extent, joined hands with Russia; for she saw to it that her soldiers shall be protected from the thing that would hinder them in their arduous fighting. (Applause.) What was England going to do? She was far behind her great Allies at present! He was pleased to see a number of letters on drink in war-time in a local paper the previous day, and also in the issue of a week before, and hoped the discussion would be followed up: On the other hand, he was very sorry that the worthy authorities of the borough did not see their way clear to pay greater heed to the petition recently presented to them. He was going to say that they seemed to pass it over with a kind of snub, but, perhaps, that was hardly the word. Anyway, he hoped that the citizens who were connected with the Temper- ance party in the town would see to it that the matter was not allowed to rest where it at pre- sent stood. (Hear, hear.) Other towns had acted. Why should Warrington be so slow? THE CASE FOR TOTAL ABOLITION. The Rev. C. P. Pirr, in seconding the resolu- tion, said that he knew that if he mentioned the question of abolition strong opposition would be brought against him. Men who were against abolition would say: If you abolish the liquor traffic it means that you will ultimately find thousands of people unemployed. He did not believe that was an argument against abolition. (Hear, hear.) On the other hand, they had a very strong case in favour of it. In the first place it would give to the nation a partial solution of many of the worst social problems of the day. In the second place it would mean the taking away of many of the -temptations which destroyed the home-life of the country. Thirdly, it would give to the child-life of the land the opportunity that it needed—it would give that which was denied to so many of the children of our land, viz., a start in life, the possibility of being properly clothed and fed. They must look at the ques- tion of drink from the Christian point of view. Anything that made a man less effective in the eyes of God and his fellow-men, anything which lowered the. standard of a man's life, anything that blotted out a man's sense of responsibility toward his fellow-men, was to be condemned root and branch. And surely strong drink came within that category? He appealed to the men and women of the town to face the drink problem—face it in the determination to solve it. If they were Christians he appealed to them in the name of their Christianity. If they answered that they professed no Christianity, then he appealed to their patriotism. If they replied that they had no patriotism, then, lastly, he appealed to the humanity within them. If they answered that they had no humanity—well, then he gave them up. (Laugh- The resolution was then put and calried with acclamation. WHERE WARRINGTON IS BEHIND! A few breezy words from Mr. WATSON fol- lowed. He pointed out that the public-houses were closed all day Sunday in Scotland, and he wanted to know why they should not be closed in England as well. ',They wanted to secure a fair chance for the Gospel by shutting up on the Lord's Day those dens of infamy which they called, more politely, maybe, public- houses. Seventeen hours every week-day and six hours every Lord's Day were those dens opened, and to him it was a ghastly shame—a state of affairs that wanted stopping at once.' In Lancaster the public-houses were shut up at 9.30 every night, and what- was done in Lancaster could be, and ought to be, done in Warrington. He said the Temperance party wanted to win for the common people—they and he—the right to shut the public-houses up Why should it be left to a handful of ma trates? (Hear, hear.) A SUCCESSFUL TEMPERANCE QUEEN. The CHAIR-LADY mentioned that Miss Eva Tanner, this year's Temperance Queen, 'had filled a high office splendidly. sc red She had f e u no fewer than 160 pledges, chiefly from boys and young fellows. She had not been afraid of using the Press to advance her work. (Hear, hear.) What she had done, other girls could do!

-- - The meeting closed with a vote of thanks to Mrs. Dodds and the speakers. During the evening the United Temperanv Choir rendered the anthems "o taste and see: (Goss) and "0 Father, Whose Almighty Power, (Handel), Mr. Spruce conducting.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE CHURCHES.

-- Temperance Sunday was observed in most of the Nonconformist Churches of the town and district. At the Bold-street Wesleyan Church in the morning, the Rev. J. Gunnell said that Russia had done a great thing in abolishing the drink traffic. If she could sacrifice .68,000,000 a year as the result of doing so, England could follow suit.—The Rev. 11. T. Dixon spoke to the chil- dren in the afternoon and many of them sign!d pledge cards.—ln the evening the Rev. H. Wil- liamson emphasised the evil effects of drink I upon the national health, pointing out that out of every 100,000 teetotalers, about 53,000 attained the age of seventy, whilst barely 42,000 out of every 100,000 drinkers reached the allotted span. Moreover, strong drink had an undoubtedly evil effect upon the morality of the nation, and prison records showed that 60 per cent. of the crimes detected could be traced to the drink curse. A special meeting was held in the Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street, in the evening, when. Mr. B. T. Parkes made an appeal to Church members to be earnest in the cause of temperance.—Several present came forward and signed the pledge. The Rev. Hugh Williamson preached on tem- perancedat Stockton Heath Wesleyan Church on Sun ay morning, Mr. A. Appleton giving an address on the same subject in the evening, whilst in the afternoon Mr. J. Pardoe spoke to the Sunday School children on the evils of strong drink. At Hill Cliffe Baptist Church a resolution was carried in favour of shortening the hours for the sale of drink during the war. The Rev. G. Curtis made special references to Temperance Sunday in his morning and evening sermons. Mr. Robert Watson, of the United Kingdom Alliance, gave temperance addresses morning and evening in the Penketh Wesleyan Church, and in the afternoon spoke to the Sunday School children on the same subject. At the Cairo-street Unitarian Church the ' Rev. Irvine Lister addressed the Young Men's ' Class on "The Necessity of Temperance," and the Adult Classes on "'The Temperance Ques- tion." To the adults he pointed out the great need for educating public opinion, and his ad- dress was followed by an interesting and in- structive discussion.

Special meetings were also held under the auspices of the Ellesmere-street Band of Hope. In the afternoon Mr. W. Harablett occupied the chair. Mr. H. Wardman, a well-known Tem- perance advocate, asked the assembly to band themselves together to stem the tide of intern- perance. He also delivered an address in the evening.

A BAD WEEK FOR GERMANY.

This, the fifteenth week of the war, has been a very bad week for Germany. That unhappy and deluded country has experienced, in fact, the worst run of luck it has so far encountered. Perhaps first place, because of its high signifi- cance, should be given to the surrender of Tsing-tau, Germany's stronghold in China, the child of the mailed-fist policy, which, a few years ago, insisted upon a place in ,the. sun for Germany. The humiliation of having to submit to summary ejectment from Asia at the hands of Japan will almost equal the loss of prestige Germany must suffer in a region where prestige counts a great deal. We may well imagine the effect this gigantic change of fortune will have upon the Chinese mind on the subject of German influence.

Next in importance comes the con- tinued successful defence of the Allies. in South-West Belgium. Three weeks of the unlimited shedding of German blood finds the Huns where they were at the beginning of their sacrifice. The quality of the material now available is chiefly poor, and admitted bravery has not made good defects in training and stamina. It will be a matter for pride in this country, and throughout the Empire, to know that the KAISER is still paying our contemptible little Army principal attention. At a time when the Germans have had to fall back upon its boys and old men against us this week the enemy has launched the famous —on paper Prussian Guards. Against the British trenches these elite of the Huns have dashed themselves to destruction, just as did the Berlin schoolboys. The defence of Ypres, we are assured, will be one of the most striking episodes in the annals of the British Amy. For three weeks the enemy has been pouring successive waves of infantry against our position, "only to see those waves break to pieces one after the ,other." This is the language of the officials; at the front, not the flowery effort of the war correspondent who has got his impressions from afar.

Nor is this all. While we have been keeping our end up, the Russians have been making runs from the other end. By a surprising, sweeping move, un- equalled in the history of warfare, covering a front of a thousand miles, they have cleared Poland of German and Austrian, and have carried the war into the enemy's country in each case. With the Cossack once again well started for Berlin, and fugitives reach- ing that place, the German mind can- not be So unclouded as .the wireless messages would have us believe. • Even the ocean is deserting them. During this same week the Emden and Konigsberg, commerce raiders that have, to a Certain extent, helped to cheer the drooping German spirits, have been laid by the heels by our Navy. Another high-speed cruiser, the Geier, has been interned by the United States. Add to all these the words of Mr. ASQUITH, speaking-for a nation and its Allies that know their own strength, and Germany will have little relish for looking back upon the Week that is now closed. "We shall never," said the PREMIER, "sheathe the sword, which we have not lightly drawn, until Bel- gium receives in full measure all and more than all that she has sacrificed, until France is adequately secured against the menace of aggression, until the rights of the smaller nationalities ol Europe are placed upon an itinassail- able foundation, and until the military

.

domination of Prussia is wholly and finally destroyed."

UNSEEMLY COUNCIL SQUABBLES. • There will be few in Warrington .beyond those immediately responsible for. them who will not echo the words of Alderman BENNETT in the Council Chamber on Monday—that he "thought they might have deferred their un- seemly squabbles for another year." Those unseemly squabbles we report fully elsewhere, so that the public may have an opportunity of seeing the light- hearted way some of our local governors treat the present serious times. The publication of the report, read in a proper spirit, may tend, in a measure, to relieve the almost un- broken cloud of sadness that has over- shadowed our doings for the past three months. To understand the squabble as reported it is necessary to have at hand a few facts which we • shall endeavour to give. The self-styled " progressives "—from both parties— wished for -many things. They wished to cut the wings of the Aldermen, Liberal and Tory, and their arguments were not always distinguished by , common-sense. For instance, they' wished to get rid of Alderman JAMES EVANS as their representative on the Asylums Board, and fairly enough argued that a doctor is a better man than a baker for that particular appointment. But they proposed that Alderman EVANS should be supplanted by Councillor CIIRIMES, a cooper. Alderman EVANS was old in the service of the Council before any doctors had come upon it, and he has done these duties well for many years. He there- fore had some right above that of the doctors, but what of the suggestion to still further keep out the doctors by a new man—and a cooper? This is only a sample of 'what happened, but it is a good one. -

===== 0002601_19141114_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OBJECT IN VIEW.'

The principal object in view, how- ever, appears to have been to eject Councillor HENRY ROBERTS from two committees—the Watch Committee and the Gas Committee—though that gentleman was not the only one to suffer.

The Watch Committee, in view of the growing demand for further re- strictions of the sale of drink during war time, is a very important body. There were five changes, the places of Aldermen PIERPOINT (resigned), SMETH- URST and BURTON, and Councillors CRAIK and ROBERTS being filled by Councillors CHRIMES, HALLIWELL, HEN SHALL, LINDLEY and DICKENSON. friendly but vigorous and justifiable but vain protest was made against add- ing Councillor CHRIMES, a licensee, to the Committee, the voting being a curious study. There voted for Mr. CHRIMES :—The Mayor, Alderman Bol- ton, Councillors Jenkins, Dickenson, Gough, Broadhurst, Halliwell, Pember- ton, Archer, Lashford, Chantler, Chimes and Cimnington. There voted against him :—Aldermen Tinnion and Pierpoint, Councillors Locker, Shaw, Wilkinson, Skelland and Lindley. So that he was chosen by 13 votes to 7. The Gas Committee was increased from 12 to 14 members. Alderman BURTON and Councillor ROBERTS were "dropped," and the following five were added: Councillors BROADHURST, CUNNINGTON, GOUGH, MeliEn and OSBORNE.

On the Watch Committee Councillor ROBERTS was of inestimable value—too valuable for some people's liking. No worthy or sufficient reason could be adduced for the action of the majority of the Council in practically dismissing him, from these two important com- mittees. The insufficient reason appears to be that Councillor ROBERTS'S ability in debate and style develops personal antagonism. After all, this is only a personal matter, and ought not to be allowed to outweigh the pub- lic interest. Councillor ROBERTS'S long experience of public affairs, his busi- ness ability and general capacity, coupled with his clear mind and thoughtful mode of expression, render him a power both in Council and Com- mittee. His rejection in this way is a loss which this curious majority had no right to inflict upon the town.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
MITTENS AND GLOVES.

Warm Hands For Soldiers in the

Trenches.

- -- - There has been a large response to Mr. Arthur Crosfield's invitation to the heads of the various works in Warrington to knit gloves and mittens to assist the Grand Duke Michael to send a quarter of a million of these winter com- forts to the British soldiers at the front. Mr. and Mrs. A. 11. Crosfield supply the wool and the workpeople or the women friends of the workpeople provide the labour. In places where only men are employed as at Rylands Bros.', the womenfolk belonging to the men form the working parties. It is not everyone who can knit gloves -and mittens, but a class at which instruction is most kindly given by Mrs. Jones, of Sankey- street, will be held in the Jelin Crosfield Memorial Hall every Tuesday evening from 7 to 10 o'clock. When the learners are proficient they will continue at home, or join their own parties, which, in many cases, are very strong. For instance, Miss Marsh has over 400 of her girls at Crosfield's already busily engaged upon this particular work. The first lesson was given in the Memorial Hall by Mrs. Jones last Tuesday night, when there was a large attendance, incluaing no fewer than 30 from Cockhedge Mill, wilere 150 are enrolled.

There was harmony, as well as enthusiasm, for throughoilt the evening the girls sang over their work beautifully, and delighted in every way Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield, who were present to witness the successful inauguration ,of the scheme.

Locally, the scheme is being managed by a general committee, consisting of two representa- tives from each of the firms providing workers, and a central committee consisting of Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, Miss Marsh, Mrs. Osborne, Mr. Willett. and Mr. Hammersley.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL-ROUND AID. .

Mrs. Edelsten reports:— The weather was probably responsible for a slight falling off in the number of garments re- ceived at the Town Hall on Thursday. Still there was a goodly pile of socks and woollies, so that a large parcel was dispatched for the use of the crew on the new submarine E 13.

Fazakerley claimed all the remainder, as the stock of the hospital is getting low, owing to the increasing stream of wounded that pours in, and again emerges convalescent, fully equipped with fresh clothing. The hospital urgently needs sheets, pants, shirts and socks, as well as suits of second-hand clothing.

Mrs. "Edelsten desires to thank the following for subscriptions and gifts: The Women's Tariff Reform League, the Burtonwood Working Party, Mrs. Fairhurst, Mrs. Tracy Forster, Mrs. Robt. Garton, Mrs. Venn, and many others.

A £17,000 SCHOOL.

Interesting Stone-Laying Ceremony

at -Oakwood Avenue.

WHERE "TOMMY ATKINS" WAS TAUGHT.

"May the task which I am called upon to perform in your name, and in the name of the governors and rulers of this town, symbolically represent the laying of the foundation of the education of the future men and women of War- rington, and may the instruction and training which will be given in these schools be such as will produce men and women capable and wil- ling to uphold the glorious traditions of our Empire, not alone on the field of battle, not alone in the world of commerce, but in the social life of the community, so that this Eng- land of ours may be not only prosperous, but healthy, happy and peaceful." With these words his WORSHIP the MAYOR (Alderman Peter Peacock) prefaced his per- formance of the first public duty which has fallen to his lot in this, his second successive year of Mayoralty. That duty was the plea- sant and memorable one of laying the memorial stone of the new Oakwood Council School, and I it was discharged in the presence of a numerous company of local civic dignitaries and educa- tionists on Wednesday afternoon.

FEATURES OF THE NEW BUILDING.

The need for a new school in this quarter of: the town has been long felt, the existing accom- modation having been sorely over-taxed. The new building is being erected on the most up- to-date lines. A site comprising in all six acres has been secured. Two and a half acres will be devoted to the school, and the. remainder will form a recreation ground for the district. Yielding to the view recently adopted by the Board of Education, the local Education Com- mittee have decided that the new school shall be built on such lines as will afford the scholars a maximum of fresh air, sunshine and elbow- room, and instead of putting up an old-fashioned building, with rooms all crowded together, they have decided that the class-rooms shall be open as far as possible on all sides to the health- giving elements. One result of this decision is the introduction of an entirely new feature So far as elementary schools are concerned, viz., the complete isolation of the assembly hall. This not only permits of the hall being used for singing and drilling and other more audible I lessons, but has enabled the committee to secure a very efficient through ventilation—admittedly the only type of ventilation which is thoroughly satisfactory. This direct access to the outside air on both sides of all the class-rooms has slightly increased the cost over the old type of school.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
TO ACCOMMODATE 1,260 SCHOLARS.

The school, when finished, will have accom- modation for 420 boys and 420 girls in one block of two stories, and for 420 infants in a separate block. There will also be another block containing a cookery-room with accom- modation for eighteen girls, a handicraft-room to accommodate thifty boys, and a douche bath, having twenty-four dressing cabins. In addi- tion there will be the usual caretaker's house, and portions of the playground will be laid out as gardens for Nature study. As water is found very near to the surface in this part of the town, the new buildings will stand on well- spread concrete footings. Upon the outside decoration of the buildings very little is being spent, the architects hoping that by a dignified treatment—good proportions and groupings and well-designed windows—a pleasing effect will be secured. Altogether it will be a school such as the ratepayers of Warrington may well be proud of. The total cost is estimated to be

===== 0002601_19141114_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
MAYORAL LUNCHEON. Queen and " Tommy's " Christmas

Box.

Prior to the stone-laying ceremony a numerous company, on the invitation of the Mayor, took luncheon together at the Town Hall, where, after an excellent repast, a few brief speeches were made.

The toast of "The King" having been loyally honoured, his WORSHIP submitted that of "The Queen and the Members of the Royal Family," and, in doing so, said that he was sure one and all would agree that, in Her Majesty, the King had a most noble helpmeet. He (the speaker). had a letter from her only that morning, signed by her with her own hand, in which she asked him to use his influence in getting together little Christmas presents to be sent to the soldiers in the trenches. The wording of the letter was simply beautiful, and it described how, when they were children, they used to look forward to Christmas as a time for receiving presents, and how they used to hang up their stockings by their bedside. Then it went on to point out how happy it would make the gallant fighting men if the people for whom they were daily risking their lives sent them little pre- sents, just to call to their minds the season and bring back to memory the Christmas joys of childhood's days. His Worship went on to briefly refer to the efforts of the Prince of Wales and said he would treasure, as a family heir- loom, the telegram which he had received from H.R.H. asking him to do what he could in Warrington to make the great National Fund opened in the Prince's name a success in War- rington. _

PRAISE FOR THE MAYOR. • Mr. R. T. PawLoran proposed the next toast, which was that of "The Mayor," and in doing so he congratulated his Worship upon the suc- cess which had attended the year of office just completed. He could say without fear of con- tradiction that Alderman Peacock had main- tained the dignity and traditions of his high office quite as well as had any of his numerous predecessors; in a word, he had made a very excellent Mayor. (Applause.) The good work he had done with regard to recruiting for the Regular and other forces of the Crown would be specially remembered. (Renewed applause.) The' toast was received with musical honours, and three cheers for the Mayor and one for the Mayoress.

HIS WORSHIP ON HIS COLLEAGUES. In responding, his WORSHIP said that whilst he had held his present office he had endea- voured faithfully to carry out the duties de- volving upon him. It had Dot always been easy for him to keep his team in order—(laugh- ter)—and whilst he had a fault in allowing too much latitude to his fellow-aldermen—(renewed laughter)—and councillors—(" Oh! Oh !")—he could assure them that he only committed that fault out of a sense of justice and equity and in order at times to bring out points for the good of the town. He did find occasionally that the aldermen—(hear, hear)—and also the coun- eillors—(" Oh! Oh !")—took advantage of the latitude which his leniency allowed them. But there was one satisfactory feature about it, and that was when the debates were over they were all good friends again. (Hear, hear.) Nothing that transpired in the Council was tinctured with spite or spleen; they gave each other hard knocks at times, but they got over it. If he might be permitted to say so, without undue egotism, he had the satisfaction of knowing that anything he had done‘had been done with the best feelings and intentions. When the time came for him to lay down his chain of office he hoped it would be the unanimous opinion of his colleagues that he had merited the confidence they had placed in him, that he had upheld the dignity attaching to the Mayoral chain, and that he had left it untarnished. In conclusion, he thanked them for the kindly thought and appreciation which prompted their cheers for the Mayoress.

WARRINGTON'S PLACE IN EDUCATION. Alderman TINNIoN proposed the health cf the Chairman of the Sites and Buildings Committee (Alderman Fairclough), and, after paying him a compliment for the excellent manner in which he and his committee did their work, went on to refer to the fact that the new school would be the fourth that had been built by the Education Authority as at present constituted, adding that he felt proud of the educational system existing in Warrington at present. There was no town in England where the educational facilities were better; where they were better used and more useful than they were in War- rington. (Hear, hear.) Provided he had the brains, it was possible for any boy in Warring- ton to commence his scholastic career in the elementary schools and finish it at the Univer- sity. (Applause.) Mr. FAIRCLOUGH, in reply, hoped that the mem- bers of the Education Committee would con- tinue on good terms, for he was certain, if they

did, that they would be able to accomplish still further good work.

EMPIRE MASTER BUILDERS.

Councillor R. HENsnALL submitted the toast of " The Architects "—Messrs. Wright and Ham- lyn—Mr. A. S. WRIGHT briefly responding, re- marking that he hoped that the new school would do credit alike to the Education Com- mittee, the contractor, and his (the speaker's) firm. • Alderman BENNETT 1141 charge of the last toast —that of "The Visitors "—remarking that the Education Committee had had previous experi- ence of Messrs. Dolan's work and knew that they built well and strongly. (Hear, hear.) The worthy Alderman went on to refer to the re- cruits who had left Warrington that day, in order to complete their training before going to the front, dubbing them the master-builders and worthy descendants of those who built the British Empire. Just in the way in which that Empire had been built, so must they build their schools; that they might stand four-square to all the winds that blow. Mr. J. DOLAN returned thanks for the manner in which the company drank the health of his firm, and gave the assurance that every endea- vour would be made to carry out the architects' ideas and to give the Education Committee the best possible value for their money.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FOURTH OF FIVE NEW

SCHOOLS.

The company then proceeded in motor cars to the site on which the new school is rapidly beginning to take shape, arriving there in the middle of a depressing downfall of drizzling rain,.from which they took shelter in a covered in portion of the playground, which is to form one of the features of the new educational institu- tion. Under cover of the convenient roof they listened to further speeches, Mr. Fatrclough, to speak figuratively, "taking the chair," and calling upon the Mayor.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR INTELLIGENT SOLDIERS.

His WORSHIP said that when the Education Committee was called into existence one of its first duties was' to consider the provision of further accommodation, and the policy was laid down of providing schools on the outskirts of the town, which would relieve the pressure on the schools in the centre of the town. The first of these schools was the Belmont School, and this was followed by the Bolton School and the Evelyn-street School, Oakwood-avenue school being the fourth of the series of five schools which have already been approved. In .1903 there were over 1,500 children under five years of age in the public elementary schools of Warrington. In order to meet the increasing numbers of children over five years of age, it had been found necessary to exclude the chil- dren under five, about 1,300 places being gained by this expedient. The increase in the child population since 1902 would amount to over 2,000, so that it would be seen that in spite of those new btildings the committee would be very little ahead of the conditions which existed in 1903. Having described the building, his Worship went on to say that in these days of difficulty and danger it was impossible to pre- vent one's, thoughts from drifting towards the great struggle which was taking place for the survival of nations, and in that connection he would like to draw their attention to a fact which had been recognised with reference to the British soldiers at the front. It was the universal admission that Private Tommy Atkins was an intelligent soldier. Had they I ever thought where it was that "Tommy" re- ceived his education? Almost entirely he was the product of the elementary schools. He (the Mover) thought that he was justified in saying that whatever might he the faults of the schools the experience of this war showed the country that they were turning out real men, who could hold their own amongst the armies of the world. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19141114_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
LAYING THE STONE.

A.t the close of the Mayor's speech the com- pany braved the elements—the unkindness of which had now somewhat abated—and moved to the site of the large foundation-stone, which was rapidly placed in position by the aid of a winch, and which was found to bear the follow- ing inscription: "This stone was laid by Alder- man Peter Peacock, Mayor of Warrington, on November 11th, 1914." Tapping it with a suit- ably inscribed silver trowel, presented to him by the contractors as a souvenir of the occasion, the Mayor declartkl the stone "well and truly laid," adding: "May the blessing of Almighty God, be with the teachers and children who will work within the walls of this school. lam sure that I feel very, very proud to have been asked to perform this ceremony, and I hope that the fruits of the education that is to be given in this school will be everlasting." Prior to the stone being placed in position, the MAYORESS deposited in a cavity beneath it a large glass jar containing current coins of the realm and copies of the local newspapers. The company then returned to the shelter in the playground, where, on the proposition of Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, the vice-chairman of the Education Committee, seconded by Councillor CFIRIMES, a vote of thanks was accorded the Mayor for his services, and where also a similar vote was passed to Mr. Fairclough, on the pro- position of ME. C. JACKSON HOLMES, seconded by Mr. GEORGE WALRER. Mr, FATamouou, in returning thanks for the compliment paid him, mentioned that he had been treasurer of the Parochial School for 40 years.

WAR ITEMS.

A WARRINGTON PRISONER. Amongst the British prisoners in the concen- tration camp at Hanover is Mr. George Monks, of 266, Lovely-lane, Warrington.

LOCAL PETITION TO PARLIAMENT. At the Wycliffe P.S.A. on Sunday members were requested to sign a petition to Parliament for the payment of £1 per week to the widows of all soldiers or sailors killed in action; or to those warriors who returned home permanently disabled.

VOCALJST HELPS LOCAL WAR FUNDS. Mr. F. V. St. Clair, the well-known singer of patriotic songs, appeared at the Palace and Hippodrome last week and sold copies of his stirring war number, "Follow the Drum," to members of the audience, the profits going to the local War Funds. As a result the sum of a little over .tll was realised and it has been forwarded to the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock).

FRENCH DECORATION FOR SOUTH LANCASHIRE MEN.

_ The President of the French Republic has honoured, amongst others, Sergt. F. J. Lumby and Pte. J. T. Pritchard, both of the 2nd Bat- talion of the South Lancashire Regiment. The decoration, bestowed upon them is that of " Medallic Militaire." Second Lieutenant G. H. Hewitt has been granted the Legion of Hongur.

ON THE SICK LIST. We are pleased to state that Lieut. and Quartermaster G. 11. Ingram is recovering from his recent attack of influenza. Lieut. Ilenshall, of the 4th Warrington Ter- ritorial Battalion, is in hospital suffering from pneumonia.

THREE GREAT FLOODS.

The earliest flood named in history is the one in which good old Father Noah figures so promi- nently. We are told that it rained so inces- santly, that the waters rose and drowned every living creature on the face of the earth, save only those who were sheltered within the ark. Another flood, of a different kind, was recently brought about by the clever engineers in gallant little Belgium. Here, to rid their country of the invading THilans, they opened the sluices, and inundated the land, so that the Germans were caught in a trap. Vast nunlbers were shot, numbers were taken prisoners, and of those who escaped we may be very sure that most of them had implanted in their systems the seeds of many an attack of rheumatism. The third, and perhaps the greatest flood of all, is Dr. Geo. E. rlood, whose efforts to find a remedy for rheumatism and all forms of uric acid troubles have been so successfully demon- strated in Liquid Barkola Compound. Many millions of bottles of this preparation have been sold, and any sufferer from gout, sciatica, lum- bago, or rheumatism in any of its forms, is heartily advised to give it a trial. A bottle costs only 2s. 3a., and the first dose usually affords almost instant relief. Boots and most other chemists stock it, or it can be had, post free, from the- Old Fort Chemical Co., Ltd., 27, Buckinghata,mad, London, N.E.

A proposal to apply for a Provisional Order compulsorily to acquire land for a school near Silver-street, has been abandoned, the Mayor being requested to see the owners and endea- vour to arrange an amicable purchase.

Emmanuel Church Sale of Work. The annual sale of work in connection with the Emmanuel Church, Bold-street, will be held in the Church Institute, Knutsford-road, on Wednesday, Thursday and Friday, November 25th, 26th and 27th.

, Lectures on "The Eternal City." Mr. Phythian was to have delivered the first of his series of lectures, under the auspices of the Museum Committee of the Corporation, on "Rome, the Eternal City; Its Story and Its Monuments," on Saturday, but, unfortunately, he was prevented from doing so by a sudden chill. He will, however, give the first of his lectures to-night, in the Co-operative Hall, at 7.30.

A Wesleyan Anniversary. The 64th anniversary of the opening of the Bold-street Wesleyan Church will be celebrated on Sunday, November 22nd, when the special preacher will be the Rev. Lefroy Yorke. On Thursday, November 26th, the Rev. Dinsdale T. Young, the President of the Wesleyan Confer- ence, will preach in the church.

"Our Debt to Spiritualism." An address on the above subject is advertised to be given in the Druids' Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening next, by Mr. Waiter Howell, of Birmingham, a prominent speaker in the Spiritualist movement. Councillor Ed- mund Osborne will take the chair at eight o'clock, and orthodox thinkers are earnestly invited.

Wesley Guild's United Rally. A united rally of the Wesley Guilds in the Warrington Circuit will be held to-morrow in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church, when the preacher, both morning and evening, will be the Rev. W. Thompson Derry. There will also be a meeting in the same church on Monday evening, when Miss Bessie Dutton will be the speaker and the Rev. W. T. Derry the chairman.

"Influence of Religion on Society." Under the above title the Rev. Irvine Lister promises a series of three striking addresses, the first of which will be given to-morrow (Sun- day) evening. The sub-titles of the addresses will be: "The Creation of Ideals," "The Crea- tion and Re-creation of Individual Character," and "The World-approaching Power of Chris- tianity."

1.8.5. A. Lecture. Jr. J. W. Palin, of Latchford, gave an inter- esting fecture at the Park Picture Palace on Sunday evening under the auspices of the Inter- national Bible Students' Association, his sub- ject being "The End of the Gentile Times." "Do 137 f," he asked, "not see the great smiting of the nations already commenced and prophecy being fulfilled prior to Israel's return to favour? But, thank God," he added, "even in this there is a blessing in disguise; it will prepare man- kind for a fuller appreciation of righteousness and truth."

Circus to be Completed. With Mr. Lloyd George's reminder in view, that he will need all the silver bullets he can get in order to beat Germany, the Town Council have this week decided to postpone the carry- ing out of part of the scheme for widening Horsemarket-street and Sankey-street, and the completion of the Circus at Market Gate, at a cost of .i200,01)0. The Circus is the portion that will be preceeded with, at a cost of , about .130,000. This will involve the purehascf of the property occupied by Messrs. J. and W. Dutton, the Blackburne Arms behind, and the Pelican Inn, Buttermarket-street.

Leather Trade " Brisk." "Business continues extraordinarily brisk," reports the Warrington correspondent of "The Leather Trades Review." "The out- standing feature of the moment is that, u hilst the demand grows dai:y to larger proportions, the supply is almost stationary, and the weight of leather coming forward is not appreciably greater than it was at the end of July. This fact Is the secret of the abnormal rise in price, and whilst the balance stands so unevenly it is useless to ex- pect any easing of the situation. Undoubtedly the supply will become greater as time goes on, but, at the best, it will lag behind while the Army orders are maintained." . For the Belgian Relief Fund. Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield are organising a grand evening concert to be given, in connec- tion with the Working Men's Infirmary Com- mittee, in aid of the Belgian Relief Fund, in the Parr Hall, on Saturday, December Uth. Every effort will be made to make the event a distinct success, both from the charitable and musical points of view, and full particulars will be announced later. As the result of a successful social recently held in the Co-operative Hall, under the aus- pices of the Warrington Women's Co-operative Guild, a sum of ..C5 was raised in aid of the local Belgian Relief Fund. A whist drive and dance in aid of the above fund will be held on Friday next, the 20th inst., under the auspices of St. Mary's C.Y.M.S The tickets are Is. 6d. each, including rsfresliments. Mr. T. Blake's band will play for dancing. A Merchant of Good Credit and

Integrity. At the Liverpool. Assizes last week Mr. Owen Toole, Liverpool, late of Warring,ton, a mer- chant in the fertilising trade, brought an action for libel against Stubbs, Ltd., London, who had issued a report, which, in his opinion, affected' his credit. Mr. Langdon, for plaintiff, said Messrs. Stubbs had agreed that judgment be entered for a sum which need not be mentioned, and taxed costs of the action. Mr. Rigby. Swift, for defendants, said that Messrs. Stubbs bad ascertained that the report was not sub- stantially correct. Under these circumstances they unreservedly withdrew the report and were pleased to state in the light of information they now possessed that plaintiff was a merchant of good credit and integrity, and they expressed regret for the wrong report they had issued.— His Lordship: A very happy result.

'Club Room for Soldiers' and Sailors'

Wives.

Next Wednesday afternoon the Mayoress (Mrs. Peter Peacock) will open a club-room specially intended for the use of the wives of soldiers and sailors at the Friends' Meeting House, Buttermarket-street, Warrington. In connection with this project Mrs. Helen M . Ransome, of Shirley, Hill Cliffe, writes us as follows:—"It will give them a comfortable place where they can read the papers or write their letters, or simply chat with their friends. Sewing and babies will be welcomed and simple refreshments will be provided at a small charge. We hope that this will supply a real want and prove the success in Warrington that similar club rooms have been in other places. May I make an appeal in your columns for help from ladies able to spare one afternoon a week to attend at the club. We hope to have it open each week-day from three to nine p.m., and to welcome any woman who likes to come." Co-operators and Pensions for Dead Soldiers' Dependents. At a recent social gathering the members of the Co-operative Guild passed the following resolution, which the Secretary was instructed to forward to the proper quarter:—" That this meeting considers it to be the duty of the State to make adequate provision for the wives and dependents of the soldiers and sailors N, o are fighting on our behalf in the European War, and expresses the opinion that the following should be the minimum weekly allowance silo- l:ated : for the widow of every soldier or sailor killed; .21 for the mother dependent en every soldier or sailor killed; a week for the wife of every soldier or sailor engaged in fighting; £1 a week for every soldier or sailor permanently maimed by fighting; ss. a week for every child dependent on soldiers or sailors fighting Or killed; the necessary funds to •be derived from the supertme'

The big attraction in next week's bill will be Gus Onlaw's Lady Boxers, who will include in their number llldlle. Carpentier (the lady champion of the world). Archie Chappell and Henry Pea's Company will present "The Touch of the Child," a dramatised version of a clever short story by the late Tom Gallon and other items in a full programme will be provided by Parks and Price, refined instrumentalists; 'Veri)., and Sid, American comedy juvenile entertain- ers; and Ed. E. Ford, "the Australian 'Sun- downer.'"

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 14, 1914.

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

OLD FAVOURITE AT THE ROYAL COURT.

That bright little musical comedy, "The Lady Slavey," is again delighting large Warrington audiences; indeed, so popular is it proving once more that one needs to get to the Royal °Court in good time if he wants a comfortable seat. Of this merry trifle—which, by the way, has been cut down into the limits of a two-hour show, so that it can be played twice nightly—it can be truly said that "age bath not withered, nor custom staled its infinite variety." That enter- prising entrepreneur, Mr. Leonard Willoughby, who tours the piece, sees to it that it is always kept up-to-date, new songs, new jokes and new dances being introduced as time goes on, though the charming little love-story that meanders through the two acts of fun and frivolity is carefully preserved. The piece is admirably staged and costumed, and the company performing it is a thoroughly capable one, carrying the piece through with a splendid swing from beginning to end. The title-role is in the hands of Miss Daisy Bray, who is the personification of all that is light and bright, happy and lovable. Mr. W. T. Thompson appears as the inimitable Roberts— the broker's man turned page-boy. Despite the fact that he has played the part well over 2,000 times, his acting is as fresh and humorous as ever. Mr. Frank H. Crimp, as Vincent A. Evelyn, the American millionaire, makes an effective hero, and his singing of the ear-haunt- ing "Must we say farewell !" and the fine patriotic ballad "Shield of Our Mighty Empire" is of that excellence 'that inevitably creates demands for encores. The part of the designing music-hall artiste, Flo Honeydew, is played with spirit by Miss Jean Lester, and Mr. R. Ireland Cutter is deliciously funny as her foppish ad- mirer, Lord Lavender. Mr. J. C. Wilton makes the most of the role of the impecunious Irish squire, Major O'Neill, and the rest of the cast is capably filled, whilst the chorus are well up to their work. Domestic Drama Next Week.

Next week, patrons of this very popular place of amusement are to have the opportunity of seeing and enjoying a successful domestic drama from the pens of Kennedy Allen and Eva Elwes, entitled "Anybody's Wife." Mr. Allen himself plays a leading part in the pro- duction.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
EXCELLENT FARE AT THE HIPPODROME.

A typical—which is another way of saying a tip-top—programme is afforded patrons at the Hippodrome this week, and crowded houses have been the order. The big attraction is a sensa- tional cowboy sketch, entitled "Silver Creek," in which Helena Millais and Co. appear. In- cluded in the short cast is Mr. William Fraser Brunner, the well-known cinema-actor, whilst devotees of the picture palaces will also notice ' other screen-favourites among those taking part in this really clever tabloid drama of life in the "Wild and Woolly West." Charles and Lily Charlene present a clever drawing-room spec- iality scene, which embraces some remarkable juggling and some nicely-rendered instrumental music; whilst Nora and Sidney Kellog extract delightful melody from a number of queer in- struments which are much more pleasing to the ear than to the eye. Another musical "turn," and a charming one at that, is supplied by Little Doris, from the London Hippodrome, who plays the violin in a manner which pro- mises a big career for her. Felix and Fisher provide a sensational gymnastic act, into which they introduce an acceptable element of comedy, and Syd Moorhouse and Arthur Boyd are a couple of good vocalists, the latter's song, "Soldiers of the Empire," being a sure applause winner. The latest edition of the " Gaumont Graphic" is projected on to the screen and includes some stirring war scenes. For Next Week.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
STAR KINEMA.

Those who visited this popular house of enter- tainment during the first three days of this week enjoyed a very varied programme, which included a fantasy, "The Devil's Drive." "The Lion" was a pretty love-story, woven. around life on a ranch. This week's " Gaumont Graphic" and " Pathe's Animated Gazette" were splendid war numbers, the latter giving an absorbing special: "Antwerp under Shot and Shell," taken from the "Whirlpool of War" Series. The principal picture,." Lily of the Valley," showed some pathetic, though exciting strike scenes; and the comedy, "When Smaltz Loves," elicited roars of laughter. _ . .

The second excellent bill of fare, finishing to- night, should not be missed. It includes a grand selection of war-films, and a thrilling drama, "In the Hands of London Crooks."

Big Attraction Ahead.

With characteristic enterprise the manage- I ment have secured for the week commencing November 23rd a film-version of the late Wilson Barrett's phenomenally successful play, "The Sign of the Cross." As the film, is reeled off Miss Marie Houghton and Madame Rathbone (of the Manchester Concerts) will render appro- priate vocal music.. Next week's attractions include pictures of Arthur Roberts in a screaming absurdity, on the first three nights, and the great naval drama, "Orders. Under Seal," at the end of the week.

PICTURE HOUSE.

Exceptionally interesting entertainments have been provided at the Picture House during the week. The principal film on the first three days was a two-reel drama of compelling inter- est, entitled "1 -lands of Horror." The two other dramas,, "Mother and Wife" and "The Girl at his, Side," were fan of incident. The Popular " Gaumont Graphic," showing in pic- torial form the latest news from all parts of the world, was of great interest, especially that part depicting the arrival of Uhlans in Ostend. The comedy element was well sustained by the three films, "Cruel, Cruel Love," " Algy's Little Error," and " Maid versus Money." Next Week. Patrons of the Picture House are promised very appetising fare next week, when amongst the excellent pictures to he shown will be the two " star " films, ".John Rance, Gentleman," and "In the Shadow of Disgrace."

NCTUREDROME.

Entertaining programmes have been the order at the Picturedrome this week, where for the first three nights "A Victim of Blackmail," a two-reel drama, was the principal film. The series of adventures shown under the title of "Lucille Love" were continued, and these two films comprised the dramatic element in the first programme. Comedy was provided by the films "Snooker's •Flirtation," "Mike Wins the Championship," and "The Fireman's Social," whilst a scenic picture, "Interlaken to Scheidegg," was much appreciated. The " Gaumont Journal" and the "singing" •pic- tute—" Everyone will be a soldier "—concluded a really excellent programme. Next Week.

Two good programmes are promised for next week, when the principal attractions will be "The Little Orphans" and "The Suicide Club."

Buttermarket-street Arbitration. Mr. Edward Holmes, of • Sheffield, civil engineer, the umpire, has made his award in the matter of the arbitration between the Rev. Joseph Howard and the Warrington Corpora- tion re the acquisition for the purpose of widen- ing Buttermarket-street of part of the premises in which the reverend gentleman has a life interest. Mr. Holmes has declared that the price to lie paid by the Corporation is ~t 1,245 which sum carries the whole of the claimant's costs. It will be remembered that the arbi- tration proceedings took place at the To`wn Hall on October 14th last. Mr. John Mainwaring was arbitrator for the claimant, and Mr. Hanson for the Corporation, Messrs. Robert Davies and Co. and the Town Clerk being the solicitors concerned in the matter.

Praise for the Mayoress. Had it not been for the fact that Alderman 'Peacock had, in the Mayoress, a very fine help- mate—(hear, hear, and applause)—he would not have been able to fulfil all the duties imposed upon him so efficiently. The charming manner in which the Mayoress had carried out her share of the work reflected the greatest credit upon her. All would agree that she had been most courteous and kindly in her manner, and that what she had been called upon to do she had I done gracefully and well. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Peacock had undoubtedly had a great task, but he had accomplished it ably and well, and he (the speaker) was quite sure that when ole Alderman's second term of office came to a close they would be able to number him among the gentlemen who had discharged the duties of the chief magistracy to the entire satisfaction of all concerned. They hoped that the coming year, under Alderman Peacock's guidance, might be a year of success for the municipal govern- ment of the good old town, and that early on in it the war would come to an end, so that the Alderman and his good lady might have a happy, successful and peaceful year. (Applause.) THE MAYOFt AND THE POOR. Councillor ARCHES seconded, remarking that there could be no doubt that the past year had been a very arduous one for the Mayor. He had done his work faithfully and well. The worthy Councillor went on to refer to the part the Mayor had played in the local Labour world, observing that no doubt his efforts with regard to the various strikes that had taken place in the town had done much to bring about Satisfactory issues in many cases. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Archer concluded with a reference to the manner in which the Mayor's associa- tion with the poorer classes of the town had earned him deserved and widespread popularity. The democratic lines upon which Alderman Peacock had worked had done much to bring him that favour which he so thoroughly de- served. Councillor Archer associated himself with Alderman Evans's tribute to the Mayoress, and said that she had brought a little ray of sunshine into many hearts in Warrington. Hear, hear.) THE MAYOR'S BIG SACRIFICE.

Councillor PrAmaToN, in supporting the pro- position, said that Dr. Joseph was a difficult gentleman for Alderman Peacock to follow, for the Doctor simply electrified the Council by the amount of time—his arduous professional duties notwithstanding—that he was able to give to the work of the Mayoralty. When Alderman Peacock was first elected, there were some people who thought that he would not be able to give the time necessary for the efficient discharge of the duties of his office, and there was no doubt that he would have had to make great sacrifices of leisure had things have hap- pened in the way everybody anticipated. But he had to make one of the greatest sacrifices that any Mayor had ever been called upon to make for Warrington. Without fear of con- tradiction, he asserted that no gentleman who ever sat in the Mayoral chair had had,, as a result, to give up the hundreds of pounds per year that Alderman Peacock was called upon to sacrifice. It was a great pity that the people he was associated with, Messrs. Crosfield, thought it necessary that he should have to give up his position with them. Yet Alderman Peacock made the sacrifice. (Applause.) But they were pleased to think that as one door closed another opened. (Hear, hear.) It was a tremandous decision that Alderman Peacock was called upon to make; no other member of the Corporation durinr, the whole time it had ex- I isted had been called upon to make such a deci- sion. In everything he had put his hand to, 1, Alderman Peacock had justified the action of his supporters in electing him to the Mayoralty. I (Applause.) ALDERMAN BENNETT'S HOPE. Alderman BOLTON desCribed Alderman Peacock as the right man in the right place. Alderman BENNETT said he thought that, per- haps, Alderman Peacock had had to face greater difficulties than had any previous Mayor, and he had discharged the duties of the honourable position he held extremely well. (Hear, hear.) He hoped that before the time arrived for the Alderman to lay down his chain of office the horrible clouds of war which were darkening all hearts would have passed away. (Hear, hear.) " THE FOUNDATION OF GOVERNMENT. Alderman Tnorios said that having loyally supported Alderman Peacock during the past year, it was his intention to continue to do so during the ensuing twelve months. The time he had given to the duties of the Mayoralty was simply wonderful, whilst the amount of skill he had put into he work and the efficient manner in which he had managed the town's affairs encouraged them to hope that, having the experience of the past twelve months to aid him, great as were his achievements in the year just ended, they would be still greater in

the widening have been acquired, and the new buildings approach completion, and I feel sure that the dignified nature of the buildings, re-

producing the Georgian architecture so eminently fitted to our town, will go a long way towards restoring the former glories of Butter- market-street as one of the foremost shopping streets in the town. (Hear, hear.) During the year a notable stride was taken in connect- tion with education. I allude to the establish- ment of scholarships to the universities for those children to whom the heavy cost is an insuperable bar. The scheme had my most hearty support, and I am glad to think it was, , inaugurated during my year of office. I could 'speak of many other notable events, but in the face of the national crisis, local affairs become of secondary importance._

„ - TOWN'S RESPONSE TO THE CALL OF ARMS. "The declaration of war with awful sudden- ness terminated the long period of peace to which we were all so accustomed. Warrington has not gone unscathed, as thousands of her sons, have willingly answered the call of King and country, and are to-day upholding the honour of the land. Arrangements have been set on foot whereby dependents are being looked after, and willing helpers are doing their best to see that those near and dear who are left behind do not suffer. We have been privileged to take our share in finding a home for some of the Belgians who have sought the shelter of our shores. (Applause.) Aye! It is a privi- lege, indeed, to help that little nation, who, throwing comfort and expediency to the irinds. ' their country devastated, their homes ruined, their cities, rich with the priceless memories of their historic past, now razed to the ground, trusting in the honour of their cause, fortified with strength, given by a higher hand than man's, have by the brilliancy of their arms won for themselves an imperishable name, and, come what may, have earned the undying gratitude of every Englishman and every lover of freedom. (Applause.) WARRINGTON PROUD OF THE SOUTH LANCASHIRES.

" The regiment with which our town is parti- cularly associated, the South Lancashire, has once more distinguished itself, and Warrington is proud of it. The splendid manner in which the men have behaved under conditions of un- paralleled difficulty has wreathed fresh laurels round their already famous record. We must not forget those who have laid down their lives during the past three months. The toll has been heavy, and our sympathy goes out to their relatives, and it is our sacred duty as a nation to see that no one who has been bereft has cause to want. Warrington has responded well to the call to arms, but greater efforts are needed, and I appeal most seriously, and with all the earnestness of which I am caPable, to our young men to come forward willing and at once, to help Lord Kitchener to break once and for all the military despotism of Germany, which has threatened the peace of Europe for the past 15 years. • RELIEF WORKS, IF NECESSARY. "Turning now for a moment to the future, Warrington up to the present has suffered very little from unemployment on account of the war. I hope this happy state of affairs may continue, but at the same time the Corporation are ready to inaugurate works to reduce un- employment should the necessity arise. We have also u,nder consideration provisional orders'for the widening of Sankey-street, Horse- market-street and Pinners-brow. The pro- posals will involve the spending of a very large sum of money—,22oo,ooo, possibly more—and it is a matter for the very serious consideration of the Council as to whether they will not post pone the consideration of these schemes for an- other year, excepting, probably, the work at Market Gate. , We have been told by the Chan- cellor of the Exchequer that the war can be waged with silver, in addition to leaden, bullets, and the Government have expressed a wish that municipalities should not compete with war loans, and that large expenditure should not be incurred except upon schemes of great urgency. The Council will have an oppor- tunity of considering the matter, but, taking all the circumstances into consideration, and as we shall probably have larger sums than usual to find when our next rate is made, the present time does not seem auspicious for the launch of any ambitious schemes. You all know my keen interest in Friendly Societies, and it is with the greatest pleasure that I learn

WARRINGTON'S WAR-YEAR MAYOR.

Alderman Peter Peacock Re-elected to the

Civic Chair.

TOWN COUNCIL'S UNANIMITY.

Year egun Aimd Strife; May it End in Peace.

The honour of unanimous re-election to the I Mayoralty of the old and flourishing borough I of Warrington was accorded to 'Alderman Peter Peacock at a special meeting of the Town Council, held at noon on Monday. His conduct in the chair during the past troublous twelve months, the whole-hearted manner in which he threw himself .into the special duties demanded of him as the civic head of the borough, owing to the war, the real and sympathetic interest which he has displayed in all movements making for the welfare and betterment of all classes within the confines of the town, have won for him the respect and ad- miration of men of all classes and of all shades of politics, and it is not every Mayor of War- rington that has been privileged to enter upon a second term of office amidst such a general chorus of praise and acclamation from his col- leagues on the municipal governing body as was bestowed upon Alderman Peacock on the occa- sion of his re-election. Having taken his seat and formally announced that the first business was the election of a Mayor, his Worship retired to an ante-room, whilst the matter of his re-election was dis- cussed and settled, and the many flattering things concerning him were said. But the Mayoress (wearing her robe of office) with a number of lady friends, remained behind to listen to the several appreciative speeches that were made, most of them containing graceful references to Mrs. Peacock herself, besides the laudatory utterances concerning the many ser- vices rendered to his fellow townsmen by her husband during his trying year of office. During the temporary absence of the Mayor, Councillor Joseph, who, during the past twelve months, had acted as Alderman Peacock's deputy, took the chair and announced that the business in hand was the consideration of the following resolution "That Mr. Peter Peacock be and he is hereby re-elected Mayor for the ensuing year, and until the acceptance of office by his successor." THE ENERGETIC AND POPULAR MAYOR: To Alderman JAMES EVANS fell the honour and duty of' proposing the adoption of the resolu- tion. He began by saying that no Mayor had ever more energetically devoted himself to the service of the town and to the carrying out of the duties of his office than had Alderman Peacock. His numerous attendances upon the various committees of the Council, as well as his attention to outside duties—duties of a social character that were beyond the scope of Council work—must have entailed' him an ex- ceedingly busy time. Fortunately, however, he possessed both the means and the time to de- vote himself to the duties of the office, and the manner in which he had discharged them un- doubtedly redounded to his credit and made for the benefit of the town generally. (Hear, hear.) One specially pleasing feature about the man- ner in which he had carried out his work was that he had known nothing of creed or party. That was extremely gratifying, especially when they remembered that his period of office must have been very trying on account of the extra duties that had been placed upon him through the outbreak of the war. Undoubtedly, under his guidance Warrington had been able to show its loyalty to King and country in a most con- vincing and creditable manner. (Hear, bear.)

the year to come. (Heart hear). They had all admired the splendid way in which the Mayor had helped on the task of furnishing forces for the Crown. He hoped that long before Alder- man Peacock's second year of office came to an end a grand and glorious, honourable and tri- umphant peace would ensue, a peace in which England would make its mark on the world, a peace which would ensure that Christian civil- isation would again be the foundation of all government. (Applause.) This was a time in which every man, no mattel• what his position or his opinions, should rally to the help of those in authority, for, by all being unanimous, they were the more likely to achieve the result at which he had just hinted, a result that was dear to the hearts. of every one of them. FRIENDLY SOCIETIES' PLEASURE.

Councillor CHRIMES voiced the pleasure of the Friendly Societies of the town at the proposed re-election of the Mayor. The members of those societies appreciated all that had been done for them by Alderman Peacock during his year of office.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR WHO CHANGED HIS VIEWS.

Councillor BROADHURST said he felt that no- body could have filled the position of Mayor more nobly than Alderman Peacock had done during the past twelve months. There was a time when he was a very adverse critic of Alder- man Peacock; when he had his doubts as to the manner in which the Alderman would fill the position, and, again, when he felt that there were other persons who had a greater claim to occupy the position of Mayor. But he must say that after twelve months' experience of Alderman Peacock as Mayor, he was perfectly satisfied that Warrington had obtained the best new blood that it was possible to get. (Hear, hear.) Councillor JOSEPH agreed with all that had been said by previous speakers, and said that though not a Friendly Society man himself, had those societies asked for the privileges which Alderman Peacock had accorded them, during his (the speaker's) term of office, they would readily have been granted. As a matter of fact, ' he believed the privileges were volunteered by Alderman Peacock. Councillor CHRIMES : That is so.

The resolution was then put and carried unanimously.

THE MAYOR'S OUTLOOK.

The MAYOR, who had been absent from the Council Chamber whilst the foregoing Speeches . _ _

were being made, and who now returned and was informed of the Council's decision, said : "For my unanimous re-election to the high office of Mayor of this borough, I thank you. It will always be my most cherished desire to take care, by every means in my power, that the confidence you have reposed in me shall never be lessened. The year that has just closed has been one of great municipal progress. The bridge, which for the last quarter of a century has furnished the Council with a never- failing subject for debate, is almost completed, and the structure, now seen in all its simple grandeur, spanning the waters of the Mersey, on a site hallowed by antiquity, will remain as a lasting monument to the abilities of John James Webster, himself a brilliant son of War- rington, and whose father occupied this civic chair years ago. It is much to our sorrow that he was not spared to see the consummation of his labours. (Hear. hear.)

MAGISTERIAL CONGRATULATIONS Mayor and Town's Splendid War-Time Behaviour.

The Mayor was congratulated upon his re- election by his fellow-magistrates at the Police Court yesterday. Mr. S. Wright said Ms Worship could rely on ' the confidence of all the magistrates. They all hoped that during the present Mayorll year peace would be declared. He hoped the people in the town would give the police as little trouble as ,possible, and so reflect credit upon themselves and the Mayor by being an orderly community. Mr. A. Browne, on behalf of the solicitors practising in the Court, also expressed the hope that the Mayor's second year of office would be even more successful than the first. Mr. G. F. F. White (clerk) associated himself with the congiatulations. In reply, the Mayor said it would be a great pleasure to him to see the war settled during his term of office. Some people in Warrington had the mistaken idea that he could control the opinions of all the magistrates, but, though he could not do that, he could assure them that he quite agreed with the opinions of the majority of the magistrates in what they had done. Warrington had behaved itself splen- didly, and, owing to the able management of the Chief Constable, they had been very respec- table, particularly seeing that this was a garri- son town. There had been a lot of imaginary complaints from people who did not know any- thing about the subject. He had noticed the town a great deal during the past three months, and was quite satisfied that many people com- plained without observing facts.

CALL TO ARMS.

SECOND LIEUTENANCY FOR SOLICITOR. Mr. Eric J. Bairstow, of Burley, Queen's Park, Chester, who was articled to Mr. T. S. Steel, solicitor, of Warrington, and who was the hon. secretary of the local Law Students' Society, has been granted a commission in the sth Battalion of the Cheshire Regiment, and expects shortly to join his battalion at the front. Mr. Bairstow has been in training for some weeks at Shrewsbury, Church Stratton, Northampton, and the School of Musketry at Newmarket. Lieutenant Bairstow previously was a Second Lieutenant in the Chester Territorials. PREPARATORY TRAINING CORPS BUSY. The members of the Warrington and District Preparatory Training Corps have during the past week been fairly well employed, having attended two evening drills and also received instruction in musketry. On Saturday afternoon last, after roll call, a route march was undertaken to Thelwall and back. The marching was exceedingly creditable and showed that considerable improvement has been made during the last few weeks. Recruits are still joining the corps in fair numbers. A great increase in the rate of enrol- ment, however, is hoped for in the near future, as it is desired to make the total roll up to a thousand by the end of the year.

post-

ANSWERING THE CALL. Over 300 Warrington Lads Enlist This Week. The urgent call for men for Lord Kitcliener's Army is not falling on deaf ears in Warrington. Despite the fact that the old town has already given liberally of her sons, others are daily coming forward and offering their services, and, if need be, their lives, in the cause of our coun- try. But, noble as the response has been, the demand is made for yet more. All infantry regiments are now open for recruits and certain other branches of H.M. Service. Recruits must be between the ages of 19 and 38, at least sft. 3in. in height, and have a chest measurement of 34/ inches; or over. Full par- ticulars may be obtained on application at the Central Police Buildings, or the Beamont Schools, Warrington. At the Police Station during the week no fewer thah 313 likely lads have registered their names. Recruiting for the Territorials is also pro- ceeding apace, about 300 :aeu having presented themselves for enrolment during the week, as against 200 for the previous three weeks. Inquiries made at the prin,;:cal works in the town show: that about eighty men have re- cr,pited, as against thirty last week. Of the total 32 came from Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' works, 20 from Messrs. flylands', eight from Messrs. Crosfield's, three from Messrs. Monks, Hall, and one from the St. Helens Cable Company.

Raising Funds for Soldiers Ga. iments. A flower show was held in the Junior Conser- vative Hall on Wednesday and Thursday with the object of assisting the war fund of the Women's Tariff Reform League. All the flowers were given by Lady Greenall, and were afterwards sold. The show was opened by Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mrs. Rylands presiding. There was a good attendance of ladies. Mrs. Greenall referred to the worthy object of the sale. She then went on to pay a tribute to the work of Lady Greenall in connection with the Unionist Women's Sewing Party. Thanks to it, she had sent about 800 garments so far. They had cost a great deal of money, and the work could not go on without help. She hoped the show would be a huge success.—Mrs. Carleton proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Greenall for opening the show, and Miss White seconded, the motion being carried with applause, Mrs. Greenall replying in suitable terms.
