MRS. CROSFIELD AS PIANIST. Shortly after the commencement of the pro- gramme, Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield entered the hall, and were accorded a rousing reception. A little later the Mayor led Mrs. Crosfield on to the platform and announced that she had kindly consented to give a pianoforte solo. "It's no use having friends," he added, " unless you can make use of them." (Laughter.) Mrs. Crosfield then gave a fine selection of music on the pianoforte, for which she was loudly ap- plauded. Mr. Crosfield then ascended the plat- form in company with the Mayoress, and was received with prolonged cheers. MR. CROSFIELD AND OUR BRAVE LANCASHIRE LADS. Mr. CROSFIELD, when the cheering had sub- sided, said he was sure that he was voicing the feelings of everybody there when he proposed that they gave a very hearty vote of thanks to the Mazoress for the splendid concert she had organised. (Applause.) They had come to look upon the Mayor as about the best record- breaker they had had in the old town of War- rington for quite a long time—(hear, hear)— but he was not at all sure that their good friend, the Mayoress, had not been a better record-breaker than the Mayor himself; after all, was quite as it should be in these, days when the mere men were getting accus- tomed, as they knew, to taking a back seat. (Laughter.) Certainly, for the last twelve, months, the Mayoress had been simply hide— fatigable in the way she had seconded the work,' which her indefatigable husband had done for' the benefit of them all. (Hear, hear.) He Crosfield) was sure that everyone there felt, as he felt, grateful both to the Mayor and his good wife for doing this vast amount of unselfish work, and for taking on these onerous duties for, a second year. They wished them well in this • further term of office. (Applause.) He would like to congratulate the Mayoress". upon the organisation of that entertainment. And he would also like to congratulate every- body upon the news they were getting from over, the water. (Applause.) He knew there was not a heart in that great hall that night which. did not beat more proudly as they considerect, the magnificent way in which all their Lane• ' shire lads had served, and were at present serv: ing, their country in one capacity or another— (loud applause)—and when they recollected how, magnificently they were adding to the laurels; which Englishmen had already gained in his- tory. It was really splendid to read the tri, butes paid to them by the greatest military' authorities of the day. They did thank their gallant lads, and their gallant Allies, too—espe, cially the brave little Belgians. (Applause.) They could all of them, in this stern and ter, rible conflict, look with heartfelt pride and joy' upon the spectacle of such heroism as they ha 4 seen, and they could glory that, in the conflict, their English soldiers and sailors had added so greatly to their reputation. A PROUD RETURN. In spite of all the hardships and of all the" danger, most of their dear lads were going to come back to them—(cheers)—and they were going to bring with them such memories and such traditions as would put in the shade even the work of their old friend, Bill Adams him- self—(laughter and applause)—and that of all the other heroes in never-to-be-forgotten

Waterloo! He said in that hall only a few weeks since that they could not find a family, one of whose members had fought in the Battle of Waterloo in the early days, which did not look upon that event as the proudest tradition in the whole family history; and those who were fighting in the great battle of Europe, and for the great cause of civilisation, at this time, would come back knowing they had a record even greater than that of Waterloo. (Applanse.) Then let them face the hardships with endur- ance and courage, knowing that at the conclu- sion of the bitter struggle there would come a great and a glorious peace, which would do much to open up a wider field and finer opportunities for humanity, and the final triumph of inter- national justice and international law. THANKING THE MAYORESS. He now asked them to join him in expressing their sincere thanks to the Mayoress for her kindness in organising that delightful enter- tainment, in the usual hearty Warrington fashion. (Loud cheers.) The motion was accordingly carried with acclamation. Replying on behalf of his wife, The MAYOR thanked them very much indeed

for their hearty vote of thanks. There was one thing which the Mayoress had not yet learned to do without—she could not do with- out her husband altogether. (Laughter.) He wished to thank very sincerely the artistes. who had so kindly given their services at the concert. " He had just learnt that Mrs.Crosfield had come to Warrington to arrange for the knitting of about 5,000 mittens for the soldiers in the trenches, by 600 girls. (Applause.) He would ask Mr. Cros- field to give them a few musical selections on the piano. MR. CROSFIELD GIVES A "TURN." Mr. Crosfield did so, and related several interesting reminiscences concerning singers he had heard, including Santley and Patti. He also referred to last week's gi'eat concert in the Albert Hall, held in -aid of the Belgian Fund, which, he said, he had the pleasure of attend-

MAYORESS AND WIVES

Entertainment For Those Left Behind.

FULL EVENINI

THE CORNISH lII\ lERA.

If all Britishers—especially those who usually go abroad at the end of the year—would recog- nise title claims of our own Riviera, much of the attractiveness of Continental resorts would disappear. Exactly the same advantages may be enjoyed in Cornwall as on the Continent. The climate is of the kind that health-seekers look for—exceptionally mild and equable. The scenery is magnificent and varied, while the social element of the seaside towns is everY whit as pleasant as that of the Continental re treats. Besides the saving - in expense and trouble, it is appropriate that at this time Englishmen should give preference to their own Riviera. A movement for all-British holidays would be consistent with the spirit of the times.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Another Ring Story. Last week's ring ease from a local hotel re- called a little experience of my own which may be mentioned here as showing how easily a ser- vant may be unjustly suspected when jewellery is missed. We once had staying with us a rela- tive who during her visit went out and bought a ring. Within four-and-twenty hours of this trausaction she announced to a dismayed house- hold that her precious acquisition had disap- peared from her dressing table. Every mem- ber of the family made diligent and repeated search, for the lost property, but for two whole and most uncomfortable days no trace of it was discovered. Likely and unlikely clues were followed without success, and when at length it was remembered that some fancy needlework had been taken out of the house for a* neigh- bour's inspection, someone suggested that the ring might have been inadvertently carried off in this and dropped in the road. The loser, however, persisted in declaring that when last she saw the article it was in her bedroom, and this left us to an alternative of which we did not like to think. In desperation I went once more to the chamber of mystery, and scrutin- ised every nook and corner with a lighted taper. While peering about on my marrow-bones, I was attracted by a glitter from beneath the fireplace, and there, just in sight in a crevice in the stonework, lay the ring. What had hap- pened was perfectly plain to the finder. The owner of the precious thing had carelessly passed the table with some wearing apparel trailing from her hand, and had swept the ring into the fireplace, where it had lodged in such a position that the brilliants alone were slightly above the surface and were not discernible until an artificial light was brought close enough for the reflection to be detected. "Verboten."

_ j wonder if, when on tour in Germany, Mr. F. Chadwick, who the other evening described his experiences to members of the Philomathic Society, suffered much inconvenience from the prevalence of the above official iiotice. The fact is that commandments beginning "Thou shalt not" are writ large all over the Vater- land, and a, paternal government rules and regulates the lives of the citizens in trivial matters and to a degree alike irritating and absurd. If one were to detail some of these, the unfamiliar reader would be amazed at the extent to which the disciplined Germans are alternately dragooned and spoon-fed by jack- booted military bullies and municipal authori- ties. And yet I do not know. Too many of our own pastors and masters here at home have of late years acquired an unfortunate habit of sitting at the feet of Germany, and deriving in- spiration from that questionable source. Preachers have sought to cram us with theology made in Germany, and in the humble judgment of the older generation of laymen the results have been mischievous and deplorable; college professors and other leaders have bidden us fall down and worship the educational fetish which Germany has set up; we have gone to that coun- try for examples in town planning, State insur- ance, and what not, as though we had deliber- ately discarded all initiative and originality and given ourselves wholly to slavish imitation of the conceited wearers of the spiked helmet and gross feeders on horse sausage and sauer kraut; and we were in tome danger of being brought into subjection to their tyrannous system of police-administered bylaws for controlling the movements of citizens from the rising of the sun to the going down of the same. It is about time we threw off this foreign yoke and deter- mined to manage our internal affairs in the good old English way. Much Over-rated. - - -

Another country that we have been taught to admire whole-heartedly and indiscriminately is Switzerland, concerning which Mr. W. H. Hart- ley had plenty that was really good to tell the Photographic Society at last week's meeting. There is quite another side to the pleasant pic- ture, and it has been exhibited by at least one who has lived in the country. "Local authorities wield more power in Swit- zerland," Miss Sellers wrote a while ago,," than in any other country. Curiously enough, they are actually allowed in many cases to act as judge, jury, prosecutor, and witness, thus com- bining four distinct roles in one. The result is, the liberty and property of the average citi- zen are completely at their mercy, and they are practically free to play the tyrant when- ever the fancy seizes them. For although every citizen has, of course, the right to appeal against them . . . . in country districts and small ' towns, no man who wishes to eat his dinner in peace and to enjoy it, ever does appeal; for he knows that, if he does, his life will be made a burden to him. The local authorities may pro- nounce him unfit to manage his own affairs, no matter how sharp-witted he may be; or they may send him to a home for inebriates, although he be fairly temperate; or to a penal work- house, although he be both self-supporting and industrious. A Swiss clergyman assured me in all seriousness that, in his district, any man— or woman—on whom the local authorities look askance must walk his shoes very straight in- deed if he were to escape being sent off to one place or another." In short, the bureaucratic government rules with rod of iron its own people; as for for- eigners, as an American put it, in Switzerland- , all foreigners rank as Egyptians, and the one Scriptural injunction that is faithfully obeyed there is that which ordains that Egyptians shall be spoiled. Further Remonstrance and Appeal. I regret to have discovered wetter blankets than those just indicated. One day this week I travelled in a railway carriage with three strangers, every one of whom, with elongated visage and in lugubrious tones, affirmed that "the British Fleet is no match for the Ger- man Navy l" I have no patience with such eickening poltroonery, and as the argument into which we immediately plunged became hotter, I had a decidedly lively time. When persons claiming to be English allow themselves to be overawed by German bounce and bluster and insignificant and easily explained reverses to our arms, and refuse to be comforted by all that one can say, not only of the glorious tradi- tions of the British Navy, but of the unparal- leled equipment, incomparable strength, and actual performances of our warships and tars, one , can only wonder at their strange constitution, and marvel that for veiy shame they do not ' keep their dismal forebodings to themselves. After all, glory be, only a,small minority of our ' people have the misfortune to he built that way; on the other hand, Jack on sea and Tommy on land dauntlessly and with characteristic cheerfulness continue to fight the foe in our behalf, and most stay-at-homes, the women especially, gladly and gratefully do their utmost to maintain the efficiency of our never-failing defenders, and to comfort the hearts and pro- vide for the needs of their dependents. Above all, the great majority never waver in the con- viction that victory will sooner or later crown these loyal and patriotip activities and self- sacrifices. The alternative to a triumph over our enemies is unthinkable by any but a mere handful of hopeless and pitiable cowards. One More Hint. A few days ago, while passing through a cer- tain Lancashire town ranking among those that have lately distinguished themselves by enthu- siastic outbursts of hospitality in relation to Belgian refugees, I beheld a spectacle which I should previously have supposed was only pos- sible in obscure corners of the Far East, where the foreigner is a rara avis—say, remote parts of China. An unfortunate little company of the town's Continental guests, having got through the preliminaries of public welcome and so on, had ventured on an evening stroll along the high-street on market day, and were evidently surprised to find themselves literally mobbed. Of course, there was nothing but their speech to distinguish them from others, but that was enough to attract the curious multitude, and they were plainly embarrassed if not positively distressed by the persistent attentions of the crowd. They endeavoured to escape these by taking refuge in a shop, but the rude crowd thronged the doorway and simply waited. The unhappy Belgians were exceedingly polite and good-humoured, but, being as I aseertained city- bred, they must have thought their gaping fol- lowers somewhat uncouth. I found it all very humiliating. and I trust it may be but an isolated instance.

IN THE COURTS.

AN UNPROFITABLE LODGER.

Stole Landlady's Son's Clothes, &e., to Buy Beer.

The story of a lodger who decamped with articles of clothing, cash, &c., while the land- lady was away from home buying food, was related to Councillor Henry Roberts (in the chair) and other magistrates at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday morning. The thief was William Lord, a labourer, of' no settled abode, and he was charged on remand with stealing from the dwelling-house, 13, James-street, a gentleman's silver watch, a jacket and vest, a pair of boots, a silk muffler, a pair of socks, a safety razor, a shaving brush, and is. 6d. in cash, the property of William Thompson. Prisoner pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable (Mr. Martin Nicholls) said that the prosecutrix was a widow, and the property belonged to her son, who was a privatt Ain the South LancasUire Territorials, and who, for the time being, was residing at home. She met the prisoner in town on Satur- day, October 31, and he told her he worked at the Gas Works, and was desirous of getting into private lodgings. He agreed to pay a certain sum each week. On the Sunday he gave 'Mrs. Thompson is. and on the following morning, whilst she was out to get some food for break- fast, he took, the articles named in the charge, and was missing when she returned home.

The son went out in search of the prisoner, and, finding him in Iforsemarket-street, handed him over to a police officer. In the short time that had elapsed prisoner had pawned the pair of boots.

- The accused, who said that he had committed the robbery in order to get money for drink. was committed to prison for three months with hard labour.

"TWO YOUNG FOOLS."

Magisterial Description of a Couple Who "Didn't Hit It."

Some outspoken remarks were made by the presiding magistrate (Councillor H. Roberts) at the conclusion of the hearing of a case at the local Police Court on Tuesday morning, in which George Donoghue, labourer, 48, Howard- street, was summoned by his wife, Sarah Lizzie, for persistent cruelty. She also asked for a separation order. The prosecutrix detailed a number of alleged cruelties committed by her husband, and stated that he had taken everything out of the house with the exception of the bed, and refused her admittance to the place. Asked how long they had been married, she replied "Two years," and, in answer to a fur- ther question, she alleged that defendant had ill-used her even before the marriage. Defendant stated that his wife had been drinking with her step-father. He "gave her good money." The Chairman intimated, that the case would be adjourned for four weeks. Defendant: I think we shall be better apart. The Chairman (hotly): The Court is not to he used to grant separations to young fools, like you two evidently are. There is something far more serious than coming here. It is your duty to live with your wife, respectably and decently, and to provide for her, and it is her duty to keep the house decent and leave the drink alone. The case will be adjourned for four weeks.

The prosecutrix made some remark which was not audible to the Press, and the Chairman, addressing her, said: "I think myself you are both very much to blame. Tell your step- father, from me, that if we get the slightest Opportunity- of dealing with him we will do it. If he is doing anything like your husband says he is, he ought to be ashamed of himself."

Ultimately it was necessary to call a policeman to him.— Prisoner, who had been before the Court twice previously this year, was fined 10s. and costs, and, in default of paying, was ordered 14 days' imprisonment.

THE

BRITAIN'S CAVE WONDERS.

Interesting Lecture to "Lit. and Phil." Society.

DOWN "THE BOTTOMLESS PIT."

Those people 'who think caves are mysterious. frightful, places haunted by all manner of dread things, are labouring under a very great delu- sion, for in reality these dark cavities contain some of the most beautiful results of Nature's work. These numerous "lights in the dark- ness" have been discovered by many brave and hardy cave explorers, one of whom, Mr. Stanley C. Phillips, of Sheffield, delighted a large nuin- ber of members of the Literary and Philosophi- cal Society in the Cairo-street Hall on Monday evening, when he gave a most entertaining and instructive paper on " Caveland wonders of Britain," illustrated by means of a host of lan- tern slides.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
A NOTE OF MELANCHOLY.

The President of the Society, the Rev. H. V. Pigot, presided, and at the outset alluded to the loss which the Society had sustained by the untimely 'death of Mr. Webster, whose work in connection with the Bridge had attracted so much attention, not only in Warrington, but throughout the country. He was one of the foremost men in his profession, and they were proud to have him as a member of that Society, at the meetings of which he had contributed some valuable papers, especially on that subject which he had made more particularly his own —the building of large and important bridges. He (the speaker) proposed that they record on the minutes their sincere grief at the loss pf such a distinguished member, who had been of so great service to society and his couhtry. Dr. Manson (the hon. secretary) seconded, and the proposition was carried.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
HISTORY OF CAVE EXPLORATION.

At the outset the Lecturer said that the opinion of many people was that the cave ex- plorer was a kind of mild imbecile, whose main object appeared to be either to make himself look as unrecognisable as possible, or else to shorten his career with a broken neck. (Laughter.) In regard to the former, he was bound to admit that sometimes their appear- ance after a hard or difficult exploration did defy description, but he trusted that his lecture would fully explain that whatever hardships the cave explorer might have to contend with, the results of his work more than requited him. Giving a brief history of cave exploration, he said that in early days, and even to-day, the belief still prevailed in some districts that caves were the haunts of dragons, hob-goblins, and other evil spirits, indeed, the arch-enemy of mankind was believed to lurk in the dark re- cesses of the cave, and brave was the man who dared penetrate more than a few yards from the entrance. The earliest chronicled explora- tion of a thorough nature was made by a Mr. Lloyd in 1771, of the famous Elden Hole in Derbyshire. The " Father" of Cave Explorers.

His report, the first of its kind, was pub- lished by the Royal Society. This gentleman had the honour of being the real father of cave explorers, but unfortunately he was a man born before his time, and the world still went on believing in bottomless pits,, dragons and evil spirits. In the early part of the last century, however, great interest was aroused in caves generally, by the wonderful discoveries made in Kent's Cavern, Torquay, and Kirkdale Cave, ,Yorkshire, showing the great antiquity of man. Such discoveries greatly helped to pave the way towards more rational beliefs, and the splendid work of Professor Boyd Dawkins seemed to give an impetus and charm to cavp work which had been felt and appreciated ever since. On Aug. 1, 1896, M. Martel, of Paris, a world-famous cave explorer, made the first descent of Gaping Ghyll, in Yorkshire, and from that time most . _

. , , ....... of the now famous exploring clubs sprang into existence.

How Water Forms Caves.

The great cave systems were found solely in the limestone districts. The reason for that was that limestone was much jointed and easily weathered. The carbonate of lime which it contained, on coming into contact with the sur- face waters, charged with carbonic acid gas, readily dissolved, especially along the joints and stratification planes. The water thus gradually percolated through the rock, enlarg- ing the fissures and joints and forming caves. Towards the close of the glacial period the streams must have had enormous volumes of water, which, no doubt, bored their way through the limestone, forming passages and chambers, the extensions and decorations of which had been done subsequently by more gently flow- ing streams. In numerous limestone districts there were many examples of streams and even rivers entirely swallowed up, to appear, perhaps, miles away from the place where they first disappeared from view.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
Great Subterranean Rivers.

In Austria and other part of -Europe this action had gone on to such an extent that large tracts of cohntry were quite waterless; all the rain water draining away immediately to feed great rivers, flowing many hundreds of feet below the ground. Fortunately the limestone districts of Britain had not yet arrived at such a state of superficial dryness. Where a large and rapid stream had swirled down a cavity, vast pot-holes had been formed, like Gaping Ghyll or Hellyn Pot, in Yorkshire. It was on the slopes of Ingleboro', in York- shire, that the lecturer, by the aid of exquisite and rare lantern slides and clear explanations, commenced, so to speak, to conduct his audience through some of the largest and most' beauti- fully decorated caves. The listeners saw how the work of the cave explorer was a dangerous one, for in descending a 360 feet deep chamber at Gaping Ghyll, the explorers had to be low- ered by a winch and wire rope attached to a' bosun's chair. By this means, the lecturer ex- plained that they could be lowered into the chamber in two and a half minutes, and he con- tinued, " There is only one way of reaching the bottom more quickly." (Laughter.)

One of the Finest Views in the Under-world.

The view- from the -;;;;;Linber, the audi- ence were told, was one of the finest in the cave- world. The explorer had, the lecturer con- tinued, also to face many hardships and dis- comforts, such as being imprisoned on account of flood for about two days, or working in a cave with water splashing on him all the time. Next were shown pictures of beautiful caves of the Castleton district, which are amongst the best known in England. Travellers so far back as the 17th century have written accounts of their visits to this interesting district. One of the clubs to which the lecturer belongs had been instrumental in throwing a great deal of light on the caves of the Castleton system. The exteriors and interiors of the Peak. Speedwell and Blue-John Caverns were shown, as were also pictures of almost impenetrable places, where the feet of man have seldom trod. Traces of the bygone miner had been discovered here, including a rough sort of receptacle for holding ore, a crow-bar, and a neatly piled up wall of stones which blocked ..up a long-forgotten passage.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
The Bottom of " The Bottomless Pit."

At the Speedwell Cavern the mystery whieh hung around the grim abyss of the Bottomless Pit was too tempting to be left unchallenged long, and so, said the lecturer, a party, in which he was included, made the descent and at the bottom found a pool, which was 22 feet deep in the deepest part. They had ex- plored the passages beyond the Bottomless Pit, where there were two magnificent cascades of water, 60 and 90 feet high respectively, which issued from cracks above the heads of the ex- plorers. In conclusion, the lecturer referred to the beauties of cave architecture and told how they were formed. The Hidden Beauties of the Cave.

• Many pictures pictures were shown of the beautiful forms of pendant stalactite and the protruding stalagmite, as well as of crystal draperies and the more exquisite cave decorations which are found in the silent pools, such as cave pearls, Sic.

The table was littered with an admirable collection of cave wonders, each of which was explained by the lecturer. On the proposition of Mr. F. G. Ruddock, Mr. Phillips was thanked most heartily for hi a ex- cellent paper. Mr. liuddock mentioned the enormous amount of work, patience and hardi- hood Mr. Phillips must have gone through to secure the wonderful pictures he had shown that night... _ . _ _ _

MARRIAGE THAT FAILED. Young Couple Separate In Less Than

A Vear.

At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, John Green, Roman-road, was sum- moned by his wife, Edith Mary, Taylor-street, for desertion, as the result of which she asked for a separation order, with alimony.—Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for the complainant, and Mr. A. Browne for the defendant.

Mr. Steel said that the parties were married on the 18th November last year and there was one child, five months old. The defendant was a file cutter, working at Widnes, but, owing to his lazy habits, there had been quarrels. He would not get up in the mornings. On the other hand, the wife was a very energetic woman. On the 10th of October he went to his work at 7.30 in the morning and his wife expected him home in the afternoon. He did not turn up on the Saturday, nor had he arrived home on the Sunday afternoon, so the wife went to her mother's house in Taylor- street. About eight o'clock on Sunday night a little girl told her that her husband wanted to see her.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
"' Locked-up' for Fighting."

She went to the corner of the street and met her husband, who said he had been locked-up in Widnes for fighting. He was under the influence of drink, so .the wife went back to her mother's house. Although he had a key of his own defendant sent to his wife for the other key, which she eventually gave him. She went to Mr. Willett, who sent a letter to defendant, and, on the Monday, she found that all the doors of the house had been locked. The land- lord was about to distrain for rent, and the furniture was on the hire system. In conse- quence of defendant not having given her suffi- cient money to keep the house going, the rent was in arrears, and, acting on the advice of Mr. Willett, she removed her clothing, wedding presents, and other things belonging to her. On the Tuesday morning she got into the house and found a letter sent by Mr. Willett behind the door, defendant not having been in. The furniture was removed and) the house was empty by the Wednesday. Mr. Browne : It was on the wife's instructions that the furniture was removed. Did Not Provide a Home. •

Mr. Steel: It was moved by the people from whom it was hired. Defendant did not make any effort to provide a home for his wife and paid nothing towards her maintenance: He earned 358. per week and on occasions had given his wife as niuch as Ms. per week. Mr. Browne argued that there had been no proof of the husbaffd's intention to end co- habitation. The man was not lazy, for daily he walked from Roman-road to Widnes, which was seven miles, in the morning, and walked all the way back at night. His average wages were only about 245. per week. It was stated that the only question at issue was that of the amount to be allowed to the wife, and the two solicitors concerned in the case wrote down the different amounts which they considered fair on slips of paper, which were then handed to the magistrates. After consultation with his colleagues, the Chairman said: "We will make an order for Bs. 6d. per week." Mr. Browne: But one of the slips named Bs._ and .the other i 75.! .

The Clerk: Ten shillings was what the wife's solicitor put down. Mr. Browne: I never heard of such a thing in my life. Mr. Steel: Mr. Browne offered 6s. and com- plainant wanted 10s. I suggested Bs. and Mr. Browne afterwards said he would make it is.

Mr. Browne: And you said Bs. • Mr, Steel: I will take, Bs. now. Mr. Browne: It is most abothinable con- duct.

The Chairman: I don't see how she can main- tain herself and child on less than Bs. 6d. Mr. Browne: The, case must go on. He said they would take Bs. Eventually an order was made on the hus- band to contribute Bs. per week towards the support Ml\ his wife.

CHRIST OR ODIN? Striking IVar Sermon by the Rev. Lev-

Jones.

F.T.C. MEMBERS AT CHURCH.

Militarism has rooted itself into the minds of the men of Stockton Heath, and even those who cannot don the khaki and line the trenches have taken up arms, prepared, if necessary, to defend their homes and their families. This body of men, members of the Preparatory Training Corps, do not "form fours" and "right turn" for show or swagger. They know it is a serious business, and, as such, they have thrown all their zeal and energy to it. The remainder of the villagers realise that the movement is not a hobby, so that it is not surprising that coneiderable interest was maZi- fested in the church parade on Sunday. The company, which was under the command of Sergt. Stirrup,. marched round the village prior to the service, the parade being headed by a number of the Church Scouts, with Assistant-' Scoutmasters Woods and Thompson in charge. 'The sermon was' given by the Rev. T. T. Lee- Jones, vicar of Daresbury, whilst the collec- tion was in aid of the Haddon Court Hospital.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
GOD'S CALL TO ENGLAND.

The Rev. T. T. Lee-Jo'nes preached from the text, "He, trembling and astonished, said, 'Lord, what wilt Thou have me to do?' " Acts ix. 6). He said that the words of the text were uttered at a crisis in the life of a great man— Saul—and the answer to his question consisted of two things—suffering and action. Saul was going along on his mad career, when he was struck by the voice of God, and trembling and astonished, said, "Lord, what wilt Thou have me to do?"

"My friends," continued the preacher, "I have taken this text because I see in it a wonderful parallel of what has happened to our great England in this present national crisis. We Englishmen were going about our business, in.our wonderful commercial prosperity, and the only question was how to get more prosperity. You know that the tendency of England before this crisis was to think of self, and the high ideal of Epglaud wag! 'Let up be comfortable 'a 1

nay, more than that, the tendency was to go farther and farther away from God." God overlooked all the races, and he believed that in His wonderful providence He had said to England, "You have to change your charac- ter from selfishness to unselfishness," "and," added the preacher, "as sure, as I stand here, I believe that this war is part of the providence of God, a bolt from the blue, a stroke from heaven, to bring us to our knees, and to bring out the finest characteristics in all English- men."

There was no doubt that they would have to suffer—like Saul had to suffer. They took up their daily papers and saw the casualty lists— England was Suffering; their very best, the flower of their national life was dying. But there was something in the war for Christ which was worse than death. That was dishonour, and that no Englishman would have. Many of them had made sacrifices.

A War Against Paganism. They had given their best—some of them had given their only sons—but they would, if neces- sary, have to give everything, for this was a war of Christ against Paganism. "And do you mean to tell me," added the preacher, "that Christ is going to be beaten by the old Saxon god Odin? It is the question in this war whether Christ or Odin is going to rule Europe, and I have little doubt of the issues."

Not only must England suffer, but she must be active. There were two forces with England which would win the battle, and these were the material and the spiritual powers. The former was at work in the field and in addition they at home were doing their part in the material way. Up and down the country there was a growing feeling that England might have to protect her- self, and as a consequence men were massing themselves in order to fight, should the neces- sity_comq.

It is 'for that reason that we congratulate you members of the Preparatory Training Corps," said Mr. Lee-Tones. " It is well to be prepared. It is no use closing the stable door when the horse has gone. Your work may never be wanted, but it is always better to be pre- pared, and your attendance here this morning is an exhibition of your readiness to stand up for the King and God of battles."

The company again paraded round the village dismissing at Victoria-square.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
FATHER'S INDISCRETION.

Picking and Stealing at a Railway Siding.

At the Stockton Heath Petty Sessions on Tuesday, two youths named Frank Hughes, jun., and Thomas Hughes, were summoned for stealing a quantity of coal, the property of the London and North-Western Railway Com- pany, at Walton. Frank Hughes, sen., the father of the two youths, of Brook Cottage, Walton Inferior, was summoned for aiding and abetting. Mr. Steel appeared for the defend- ants.

Superintendent Owen said that on November Ist P.C. Rowlands met the younger prisoners in Walton Inferior. Each of them was carrying a bag. When asked what was in the bags they answered " Coal." Further questioned, they said their father had picked the coal up and did not like to bring it home on Sunday morning, so they had been sent to fetch it. The father admitted that he sent the boys for the coal. There was, the witness added, a waggon load of coal in a railway siding, and there was evidence that the coal had been taken from it.

Mr. Steel said the facts were not disputed at all. Mr. Hughes saw the coal and told his boys to pick it, as he did not think there was any harm in it. He now admitted that he had made a mistake in thinking that, bCcause he had seen others doing it, he was entitled to do it. He took the responsibility on his own shoulders.

The Chairman said the father had been guilty of an indiscretion and would be fined 405., in- cluding costs. The boys would be given the benefit of the doubt and the caes against them would be dismissed.

LYMM.

Commission for a Lymm Licensee.—At the Altrincham Petty Sessions on Monday, before Mr. J. W. Sidebotham and other justices, the licence of the Golden Fleece Inn, Lymm, was transferred from Mr. James Willcock to Mr. John Smith Thorley, of 69, Legh-street, War- rington.—Mr, T. S. Steel, of Warrington, in making the application, said that Mr. Willcock had hoped to spend the regt of his life at the Golden Fleece at Lymm, but having been a sergeant-major in the Volunteers, and knowing that.: his King and country were in need of trained men, he had offered his services for the present war. Such services had been accepted by the Government, and Mr. Willcock had been granted a commission, and consequently it was necessary for him to give up the licence.

Fifteen Wounded Britishers. Fifteen wounded " Tommies" are receiving treatment at Brookfield House, Lyinm, which has been converted into a British Red Cross Hospital. The house has been kindly put at the disposal of a local committee and everything possible is being done to make the wounded soldiers com- fortable. For some weeks the nurses and others who have volunteered their services have been busily engaged in making preparations, and when the men arrived on Monday evening everything was in readiness for their reception. One of tlAf men, Private W. Thackeray, of Lon- don, and a member of the Royal Field Artil- lery, received his injuries through the explosion of a bomb dropped from an aeroplane. Another man, who came over from India with the native regiments, is suffering from an injury to his wrist, caused by the bursting of a shrapnel shell.

PADGATE.

District Nursing Association.—A jumble sale in aid of the above, held in the Church Schools on Friday of last week, realised about £l7.

Wesley Guild.—Before a good attendance, of the members Of the Guild on Monday evening, Mrs. A. J. Morton read a paper on the life and works of Charlotte Bronte, one of the brilliant novelist-sisters, whose works so enriched Vic- torian literature. Miss Ward presided.

Service of Song.—A service of song entitled "The Roll Call" was rendered by an augmented choir in the Wesley Hall on Thursday evening. The service was illustrated 'throughout with lan- tern slides, and a list of those who have left the Padgate district and joined the colours was shown on the screen. The reader was Mrs. A. J. Morton, of Padgate, and the chairman Mr. J. E. Richardson, of Frodsham. The soloists were the Misses B. London, J. Ward, M. Apple- ten, Mrs. Bate and Mrs. Plinston, and a duet, entitled " 0 lovely peace" (Judaa Maccabms), was very ably rendered by Mrs. J. Bennett and Miss Mason. The choir, who were elnder the conductorship of Mr. W. J. Bate, gave a good account of themselves, and at the close of the service sang the " Hallelujah chorus." The pro- ceeds were in aid of the trust funds.

1 Wesleyan Aid for Belgian Refugees.—An important church meeting was held on Tuesday Presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. For some time there have been large demands on the church resources, ,and after some discus- sion an appeal was made for financial help, the call being ,very generously responded to by the church meinbers, a total amount of £5O being promised at the meeting. The renovation of the church was also discussed, but it was decided to postpone this for the present. It was also agreed to support a Belgian family of not more than six to eight persons, in a house kindly placed at the disposal of the church by Mr. Bennett, and the members offered to contribute in small sums weekly an amount sufficient to meet the expenses. An ,appeal for surplus articles of furniture was also made, and fur- ther offers are still required in order to furnish the house and make it comfortable. The furni- ture will be returned after the war is over and the Belgians return to their own country.

HATTON.

Village Lads Rally to the Flag.—The little village of Hatton has need to be proud of her share in the national recruiting movement. Out of her tiny population she has given the follow- ing to the country's service: H. Leach (London Regt. Territorials); T. Rutter, T. Houghton, F. Cliff e and J. Buckley (Army, Service Corps); C. Davies, P. Chile. W. Green and F. Smith (King's Royal Rifles); J. Kippax (Army Ambu- lance Corps); and. T. Dutton (Royal Navy). Other villagers are doing duty on the Civil Guard, under the leadership of Mr. J. J. Whit- ley.

DESERTION GASES:

A Runaway Not Wanted By His Regiment.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday John Mills was charged with being an absentee front the 27th Battery R.F.A., stationed at Bulford since the 27th October.—Acting-Sergeant Arm- strong said he saw the prisoner in civilian dress, and, knowing that he had enlisted, he asked him what he was doing in Warrington. Prisoner said he had lost his pass and his uniform at Bristol. At the Police Station lie admitted he was a deserter.

The Chief Constable asked for an adjourn- ment for seven days. He was satisfied that the regiment would not want the prisoner back, for he had been doing harm to recruiting generally. He was under the influence of drink and had made allegations which he ought not to have made. The Commanding Officer had been com- municated with, and it was probable that pri- soner would be discharged from the Army. He had made most serious allegations, which ought not to go unchallenged.

Prisoner was remanded for seven days. DESERTED REGIMENT AND CHILDREN.

At the Police Court pn Friday, before the Mayor (in the chair),. Aldermen Pierpoint, Bolton, Bennett and James Evans, Messrs. S. Wright, W. Furness and A. J. Jolley, Thomas Sandon, or Henley, was charged with being a deserter from the 3rd Battalion of the King's Liverpool •Regiment—Mr. A. Browne said the prisoner was also charged with neglecting his three children, but they had not been able to find him until that morning. He requested that that case be adjourned sine die.—The mag- istrates decided to hand prisoner over to an escort, and adjourned the case of neglecting his children sine die. TWICE AN ABSENTEE.

Herbert Sherratt was ordered to be handed over to an escort at the Borough Police Court 3n Monday, when he was charged with being ita absentee from the 3rd Battalion South Lan- cashire Regiment (stationed at Crosby) since the 24th October.

Detective-Sergeant Reddington gave evidence 89 to arresting the prisoner, and the Chief Con- stable mentioned that he had been handed over to an escort on October 20th, when he was charged with a similar offence.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
WHY HE ABSCONDED.

"If Kitchener wants men, let him pay them," was a remark made at the Borough Police Court on Wednesday morning by Samuel Myerscough, a private in the Royal Army Medical Corps, who was charged with being a deserter since Sep- tember 25th.—Detective-Sergeant Holland said that he visited the house, 98, Evelyn-street, at 10 o'clock the previous night and charged the prisoner with being a deserter. This he stoutly denied, but when he was taken to the Police Station he admitted it. Prisoner made the re- mark already quoted, and stated that 15 of them deserted at the same time as they could get no money, not even enough to buy soap. He was was ordered to he handed over to the military escort on its arrival. ANOTHER ABSENTEE.

At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, George Worthington, a private in the 3rd Battalion of the South Lancashire Regiment, which is stationed at Great Crosby, was charged with having been an absentee since October 17th and was ordered to be detained until the arrival of the escort.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
OVERSTAYED HIS LEAVE.

At the-san-le-dirWrC;n-Wednesday morning, Edward Clifton, a private in the 9th Battalion Lancashire Fusilier, stationed at Grantham, was charged with being an absentee from his regiment, and on his promising to return straight to his headquarters he was allowed to go without an escort, the Clerk stating that he had overstayed his leave. ===== 0002601_19141114_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
A CALL TO THE WOMEN. How They Can Help Whilst the Men are Fighting.

At a time when political work is at a stand- still there is naturally much dormant energy that can be usefully turned into other channels. Miss Blythe, of the Women's Liberal Federa- tion, London, is one of the many who have seized this opportunity. She is at present en- gaged upon a tour of the Women's Liberal Asso- ciations in Lancashire and Cheshire, giving addresses and pointing out how good work may be done. She recently called upon Mrs. Osborne, the honorary secretary of the local association, to ask what the women of Warrington were doing. She was promptly invited to one of the war sewing parties at the John Crosfield Memorial Hall, and was delighted with the work that she saw in progress.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
A CALL TO EVERY WOMAN.

Miss Blythe, in the -course of an address to the workers, said that owing to the tyrannical and dishonourable behaviour of the German Emperor and his War Lords, this country was plunged into the most awful war in her history and her people had to face consequences and disasters that, she feared, would not only con- tinue, but would be greatly increased as time went on. They were fighting a terrible foe without the borders of their country, and they would be called upon to fight a terrible foe within them. The fighting of the foe without was more particularly men's work; the fighting of the foe at home was within women's province and the call came to every woman to do her part. They felt proud of their men for being willing to sacrifice themselves in order to carry on the fight to such an extent that war again should be impossible, but whilst they were, with hopeful hearts, looking forward to a time of real peace when this war was ended, they knew that between now and that time they had to pass through a period shadowed by terrible clouds. They. had the satisfaction of knowing that the 'responsibility for the war did not rest on their shoulders. SACRIFICES IN A GREAT CAUSE.

It was for the great cause of real Progress that women were giving up their men, some for a time, some for a life-time. This nation could not and would not stand by and see a smaller and weaker nation wronged and ill- treated by any manifestation of brutal force, for it felt that to do so would make it an ac- complice in the brutality. That was Britain's position under the moral laws, putting all question of treaties aside. •The men were doing their share; what were the women doing? The speaker went on to indicate directions in which they might help, and referred to the fact that through the agency of Queen Mary's Fund it had been possible to start workrooms for sewing new materials and renovating cast-off garments, and also for the making of simple toys to take the place of the German toys. A step-cleaning scheme for older women and a jam and pickle- making industry were among the suggestions that had been put forward.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Involved Areas.

FIFTEEN'

EI WEEK.

HACKING.

BUT NOT THROUGH TO CALAIS.

We have ,had another week of German en- deavour to hack a way through to Calais, but on the whole the Alliesi have made the greater amount of progress n spite of the awful losses to which the Germans have subjected them- selves. _ a _

A despatch from the eye-witness present with General Headquarters describes the fighting during the early days of the present month. It shows how hard the Britisii troops have been pressed and how well they have stood the strain. Of late there have Wen among the pris- oners taken many untrained boys, wllo had never fired a rifle before they went into iTib—cbn- tinning _battle:.

British artillery destroyed a German heavy battery a fortnight ago, while an enemy's aero- plane was captured undamaged. The Germans still report upon their "young" army in Flanders.

Early in the week the Belgians resumed the offensive among the dunes of Nieuport. Fighting near Ypres led to an advance on the part of the Allies. In the fighting on Tuesday between Armen- tieres and the sea the Allied army gainer/Ovine advantages. The British positions still had to sustain the full fury of the enemy's assault, which was "energetically repulsed."

Summary of Authentic News From Ail

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR "THE EXAMINER."

SOUTH AFRICA.

BOTHA DEFEATS DE WET.

The South African rebels are increasingly unfortunate. Many have returned to their homes, declaring they had been misled, many others have been captured, and some have been killed. _ _

On November 8 (Sunday) Lieut.-Colonel Dirk Van De Venter came into contact with the rebels at Zandfontein, near Warmbaths, 64 miles north of Pretoria. Of the enemy 126 were killed and wounded and 25 were captured. Colonel Van De Venter lost 12 killed and 11 wounded. General Botha came into contact with De Wet's commando east of Winberg after a forced night's march. The rebels were severely de- feated and 250 prisoners taken and two laagers captured. The rebel general Chris Muller has been cap- tured. He was found in a farmhouse 60 miles north of Bronkhorst Spruit -wounded in both legs. lie was brought to Pretoria in a motor car.

It is reported by Commanilers Greyling and Ross that they had a short fight with the rebels in the Northern Orange Free State. Fifteen prisoners were taken, one being severely wounded, whilst another was slightly wounded. Our forces suffered no casualties.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANS CAPTURE DIXMUDE.

On Wednesday the Allies re-occupied Lom- bartzycle, north of Nieuport, and made pro- gress beyond this place, but at the eiid of the, day the Germans succeeded in taking possesssion of Dixmude. The British troops, who were attacked at several points, everywhere checked the enemy. The enemy throughout Wednesday, says the Paris official report, continued his efforts of yes- terday without achieving any fresh result. He directed against Lombartzyde a counter-attack, which was repulsed. He made vain attempts to debouch from Dismude on the left bank of the Yser._

_ Paris also notes that "speaking generally our front has been maintained." This claim agrees with what is known of the main outline of the Allied front.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
PRUSSIAN GUARDS REPULSED BY THE

BRITISH.

—..—... The section. of thi. s l i.ne which is held by the British Army was again subjected to fierce at- tacks on Wednesday. Among these, an attempt made by a body of the Prussian Guards is singled out for special mention. All have been repelled. _ . _ _

The centre of the struggle has been Ypres, the defence of which will certainly be reckoned in history as one of the most striking episodes in the annals of the British Army. For more than three weeks that position, which pro- jects like a bastion into the enemy's lines, has been held under a rain of shells which has hardly ceased by day or night. During this time the enemy has poured successive waves of infantry against it, only to see them break to pieces one after the other.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
THE AISNE FRONT.

DESTRUCTION OF GERMAN GUNS.

On the _Aisne line the positions retaken by the French near Vailly some days ago have been held in face of a fierce counter-attack. The Allied line has again been pushed for- ward around Berry-au-Bac, and in the region of Craonue the artillery of the Allies came suc- cessfully out of a duel with that of the enemy. Some of his guns were destroyed.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY INVADED.

RAPID RUSSIAN ADVANCE.

The Russian advance has been exceedingly rapid. .

The Germans abandoned Czestochowa and Kalisch, and the Russians successfully invaded the Province of Posen in Prussian Poland. Advancing from Lodz against the left wing of the German army beaten before Warsaw, the Russians forced a way over the river Wartha, inflicting while so doing considerable losses on the ,enemy:_

In East Prussia the Russians pressed forward, driving the Germans before them. In Galicia the Austrians abandoned all their positions along the lower San, and were re- treating into Hungary. Many guns and 30;000 prisoners fell into Russian hands. The Russian General Staff announces that the right wing of the German Army in East Prussia has been defeated. Along the whole front the Russian advance continues. Russian cavalry are near Kalisz, on the Western frontier of Russian Poland, and have come within 20 miles of Cracow.

Przemysl is again hedged round by Russian troops. Strong detachments are moving north- west, west, and south of it. The losses of the Austro-German Armies in the battle of the Vistula exceeded 100,000 men. More than 22,000 Germans were taken prisoners as a result of the victory on the East Prussian frontier, besides 52 guns, 50 machine-guns, and countless quantities of ammunition and provi- sions.

Yesterday's reports showed the Russians were well over the frontier of East Prussia, within striking distance of Thorn. They were threat- ening Silesia, where the people were said to be already taking to flight. The Russian advance upon the Austro-Ger- man armies continues. In Western Poland, in East Prussia, and against Cracow the troops are moving forward. Among the Carpathian foothills the Dual Monarchy's forces are hard pressed. Berlin and Vienna both admit the reinvestment of Przemysl.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
TURKEY.

In the Persian Gulf H.M.S. Odin and a small British force oecupied Fao, the last Turkish town on the Shat-el-Arab, after an hour's fight- ing. The Admiralty statement says a boat from the Ocean co-operated in the naval operations. Two Turkish divisions with cavalry and guns have been routed and put to flight by the Rus- sian troops invading Armenia. The fighting occurred between the frontier and Erzerum, towards which important Ottoban military centre the invaders are advancing. Four Ottoman transports oft the Black Sea coast of Asia Minor have been caught and Sunk by Russian warships. The lost vessels had on board troops, guns, rifles, ammunitiai, uni- forms, motor cars, aeroplanes, and stores for the army in Arna'inia. Russian and Turk both claim victories on the European frontier of Turkey. The Russian communique is the more modest in its claims. It assigns the offensive to the Turks, but describes the ruin of their attempts to outflank the invading Russian army.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0165.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN CRUISERS • LOST.

END OF THE EMDEN.

The Emden appeared off Cocos Island, in the Southern Indian Ocean, and landed a party to cut the cable and destroy the wireless station. Before' her work had been done. H.M.S. Sydney arrived. Forced tO fight, the Emden was dam- aged, driven ashore, and destroyed. The captain of the Emden and Prince Franz Joseph of Hohenzollern are both prisoners and unwounded. The losses of the Emden in killed are reported unofficially as 200, with 30 wounded. .Another German cruiser, the Konigsberg, has been discovered by H.M.S. Chatham hiding in a river mouth on the coast of German East Africa..

The Sydney is one of the Australian navy's light cruisers. The German cruiser Geier, having overstayed the welcome properly accorded to an injured belligerent at Honolulu, has been interned by the United States authorities. With her was the German steamer Locksun, believed to be a naval tender, which has shared her fate.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH GUNBOAT TORPEDOED.

The British torpedo-gunboat Niger, a small vessel of little value belonging to an obsoles- cent class, was sunk in the Downs by a German submarine. Most of the crew were rescued. Crowds on the Kent coast saw the Nige• go down.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S SUN

SETS IN THE FAR EAST.

Tsingtau, the port of -Kiao-chau, has fallen to the Anglo-Japanese investing force. Thus Ger- many—losing her only possession in the East—is expelled from Asia. It is officially announced in Tokio that tor- pedo-boat No. 33 has been sunk by a mine in the mouth of Kiao-chau Bay. The majority of the crew have been saved. The torpedo-boat was dragging for mines at the time.

===== 0002601_19141114_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
CAMEROONS.

EXPELLING THE GERMANS FROM AFRICA.

Our French Allies announce that the brilliant operations of a column under General Ayme- rich have resulted in the expulsion of the Ger- mans from a large part of the Eastern Comer- oons, near the Congo. Most of the territory which the Germans ex- tracted from the Republic during the negotia- tions after the Agadir coup in 1911 now returns to French control. .

British troops from Nigeria are busy in the old Cameroons. .

===== 0002601_19141114_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
57,000 CASUALTIES.

Mr. Asquith states that the British casual- ties up to October 31, are approximately 57,000 of all ranks. This number will include missing and wounded.

' Any Insured Persons who desire to change their DOCTOR or INSTITUTION, or the method by which they have been obtaining medical treatment, as from the Ist January next, must give notice to the Committee before the Ist DECEMBER next. WHIST FOR THE WAR FUNDS. Row Liberal Women Raised £25. The Liberal women of Warrington consider it one of their first duties to do all they can to alleviate suffering, and since the outbreak of the war their usually praiseworthy activities in this direction have been increased. Individu- ally, as well as collectively, their efforts have been untiring, and on Thursday evening a sum of 0625 was realised in aid of the Local War Fund and the Fund in aid of those who man the mine-sweepers, by means of a highly suc- cessful whist drive and dance, held in the John Crosfield Memorial Hall. Mrs. Locker and Mrs. Lindley had made excellent arrangements, and bore the cost of the whole affair, including re- freshments and the whist prizes. There were over 200 ladies and gentlemen present, includ- ing the Mayor and Mayoress and several leaders of the Liberal party. The whist prize-winners were :—Ladies 1, Mrs. Scott; 2, the Mayoress; 3, Mrs. Stanley Walker. Gents: 1, Mr. F. E. Bibby; 2, Mr. Arthur Greenhalgh; 3, Mr. F. Walker. The hidden prizes for those who had gained the number of marks equal to the result of a division of the highest and lowest numbers added together, were won by Mrs. Eaton and Mr. Barber. Mrs. Tinnion prPsei,tec the Mayoress with her prize, and then the Mayoress handed the other prizes to the successful players. The M.C.'s for dancing were Councillor Lind- ley and Mr. T. Locker, whilst Mr. G. Lomas discharged the duties of accompanist in an effi- cient manner. The total sum realised included £1 2s. 6d., part proceeds of a draw for a beautiful collec- tion of chrysanthemums grown and presented by Mr. H. Gorst, of Manchester-road.

PROGRESS OF THE WAR.

" Mean " Insurance Commissioners. At a meeting of the Warrington Insurance Committee on Thursday evening, when the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) presided, Councillor Joseph gave a report of a meeting of the Association of Insurance Committees, in which he stated that the Insurance Commis- sioners still stuck to the same mean style of treatment with regard to the payment of mem- bers of Insurance Committees for attending Meetings. The treatment, he said, had ex- tended to members of the Exdcutive Committee, who attended in London. They were allowed ss. for a day, and 15s. if they were in London all night. That was what was recommended, and 10s. 6d. and 12s. 6d. if in some provincial town. That was, he thought, too little, and anyone submitting to it would lose very mate- rially by it. STOCKTON HEATH St. Thomas' Band of Hope.—The members spent a pleasant evening together on Thursday, when Mrs. Wheeler entertained them with a selection of choice gramophone records. The officers wore their new regalia for the first time. and Mesdames Hallows and Newall were thanked for making it. PENKETH.. Wesley Guild's Musical Evening.—A good attendance of members turned up at the meet- ing on Tuesday evening, and thoroughly en- joyed an excellent musical programme arranged by Mrs. James Dodds. The artistes were: Mrs. Dwerryhouse, the Misses Hilda Baker anil Alderney, and Messrs. F. Hall, S. Plumpton and Paden.
